                                               COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE
                                                  FORM AND TRACE-FREE MULTIPLICATION ENDOMORPHISMS




arXiv:2004.12343v2 [math.RA] 14 May 2020
                                                                                                DANIEL J. F. FOX


                                                   Abstract. A commutative algebra is exact if its multiplication endomorphisms are trace-free and is Killing
                                                   metrized if its Killing type trace-form is nondegenerate and invariant. A Killing metrized exact commutative
                                                   algebra is necessarily neither unital nor associative. Such algebras can be viewed as commutative analogues
                                                   of semisimple Lie algebras or, alternatively, as nonassociative generalizations of étale (associative) algebras.
                                                   Some basic examples are described and there are introduced quantitative measures of nonassociativity,
                                                   formally analogous to curvatures of connections, that serve to facilitate the organization and characterization
                                                   of these algebras.

                                                                                               1. Introduction
                                              A commutative algebra (A, ◦) is exact if its multiplication endomorphism L◦ (x) ∈ End(A) defined by
                                           L◦ (x)y = x ◦ y are trace-free, and is Killing metrized if its Killing form τ◦ (x, y) = tr L◦ (x)L◦ (y) is nonde-
                                           generate and invariant. Such an algebra has no unit and is not associative. This article provides evidence
                                           that the class of Killing metrized exact commutative algebras is a tractable commutative analogue of the
                                           class of semisimple Lie algebras, and introduces a quantitative measure of sectional nonassociativity, formally
                                           analogous to sectional curvature, that generalizes the usual Norton inequality and can be used to organize
                                           the study of such algebras and, in some particular cases, to characterize and classify them.
                                              Throughout the article the base field is denoted k and k× is the multiplicative group comprising its units.
                                           Where not specified the characteristic of k is assumed to be zero, although this is stated explicitly where
                                           necessary. For some constructions that depend on positivity it is assumed that k = R.
                                              An algebra means a k-vector space A equipped with a k-bilinear map ◦ : A × A → A that is called the
                                           multiplication. It need not be either unital or associative. In this paper all algebras are finite-dimensional as
                                           k-vector spaces, and this assumption is not repeated.
                                                                                                          1
2                                               DANIEL J. F. FOX


   Let (A, ◦) be a commutative algebra. The image L◦ (x) of the linear map L◦ : A → Endk (A) defined by
L◦ (x)y = x ◦ y is called the multiplication endomorphism determined by x ∈ A.
   A bilinear form h ∈ ⊗2 A∗ is invariant if for all x, y, z ∈ A,
(1.1)                                       h(x ◦ y, z) = h(x, y ◦ z).
The algebra (A, ◦) is metrized if there is a nondegenerate invariant symmetric bilinear form h ∈ S 2 A∗ (a
metric). For background on metrized commutative algebras see [10, 66].
   Provided char k is relatively prime to 6, the cubic polynomial P ∈ S 3 A∗ of the metrized commutative
algebra (A, ◦, h) is defined by defined by 6P (x) = h(x ◦ x, x). The multiplication of (A, ◦, h) is determined
completely from its associated cubic polynomial via polarization by
(1.2)      h(x, y ◦ z) =P (x + y + z) − P (x + y) − P (y + z) − P (x + z) + P (x) + P (y) + P (z).
Metrized commutative algebras can be studied by applying techniques from analysis and invariant theory to
the cubic polynomial [27, 66].
   That a commutative algebra be metrized means that its multiplication endomorphisms L◦ (x) are h-self-
adjoint. This has strong consequences for the spectral properties of multiplication endomorphisms L◦ (x). If
k = R and h has definite signature, it means that the L◦ (x) are semisimple with real eigenvalues.
   Despite its good features, the class of metrized commutative algebras is too general to admit a good
structure theory. In order to have some hope of reasonable classification and characterization some further
conditions need to be imposed. One possibility is to link the invariant metric to the algebra structure in
some a priori fashion. The conditions considered here are that certain particular trace-forms, the Ricci and
Killing forms, be invariant and nondegenerate.
   For a Lie algebra the Killing form is automatically invariant. For a Jordan algebra over a field of char-
acteristic not equal to 2, the trace-form tr L◦ (x ◦ y) is automatically invariant [72], but the Killing type
trace-form generally is not invariant. For a commutative algebra the invariance of some symmetric bilinear
form is in general not automatic and the choice of the particular trace-form required to be invariant or
nondegenerate matters and conditions requiring such invariance or nondegeneracy should be regarded as
part of the algebraic structure. To explain this more precisely, some definitions are needed.
   An automorphism of an algebra (A, ◦) is an invertible endomorphism φ ∈ End(A) such that φ(x◦y) = φ(x)◦
φ(y). The automorphisms of (A, ◦) constitute a closed subgroup Aut(A, ◦) of GL(A). An automorphism of a
metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) is an algebra automorphism that preserves h. The group Aut(A, ◦, h)
of automorphisms of (A, ◦, h) equals the intersection Aut(A, ◦) ∩ O(h), where O(h) is the orthogonal group
of h.
   By a trace-form is meant a multilinear form constructed from powers of multiplication endomorphisms
and the multiplication itself. The importance of trace-forms is that they are automorphism invariants. For
a general discussion of trace-forms from the point of view of invariant theory see [70].
   Any linear trace-form is a multiple of tr L◦ (x), so the only interesting general condition involving linear
trace-forms is that tr L◦ (x) = 0 for all x ∈ A. A commutative algebra (A, ◦) is exact if tr L◦ (x) = 0 for all
x ∈ A. Exactness of a commutative algebra is analogous to the unimodularity of a Lie algebra. Here it serves
as a helpful simplifying assumption, in the same sense that restricting to unimodular Lie algebras excludes
the wilder solvable Lie algebras.
   The most general bilinear trace-form is a linear combination of the form
(1.3)                       α tr L◦ (x)L◦ (y) + β tr L◦ (x) tr L◦ (y) + γ tr L◦ (x ◦ y).
The invariance or nondegeneracy of a particular trace-form are additional structural conditions not satisfied
by a generic commutative algebra. In the presence of the exactness condition the only possibility is the
Killing type trace-form
(1.4)                                      τ◦ (x, y) = tr L◦ (x)L◦ (y).
A commutative algebra is Killing invariant if its Killing form is invariant, and is Killing metrized if its
Killing form is invariant and nondegenerate. For a Killing metrized commutative algebra, an automorphism
necessarily preserves the Killing form, so in this case Aut(A, ◦) = Aut(A, ◦, τ◦ ).
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                   3


   The invariance and nondegeneracy of the Killing form of a Killing metrized commutative algebra can be
further strengthened. Over a general base field k, τ◦ can be supposed to be moreover anisotropic, and when
k = R (or, more generally, k is real closed) it can be supposed to be positive definite. Stronger results are
obtained in these settings.
   One motivation for considering the class of Killing metrized commutative algebras is that it can be
regarded as a commutative analogue of the class of semisimple Lie algebras. The semisimplicity corresponds
to the nondegeneracy of the metric. On the other hand, the invariance of the metric is an independent
condition that, while automatic for Lie algebras, must be imposed in the setting of commutative algebras.
This suggests that relaxing the nondegeneracy condition could be interesting, and this is the reason for
delineating the more general class of Killing invariant commutative algebras.
   The relation with Lie algebras is in fact stronger than mere analogy as the tensor product of simple
Lie algebras is a Killing metrized commutative algebra; see Lemma 2.8. The class of Killing metrized
commutative algebras is essentially the smallest that contains the algebra tensor products of semisimple Lie
algebras (a precise formulation is not given here). This alone gives good motivation for studying metrized
commutative algebras. Further motivation is given by the identification of interesting special examples such
as those described here in Sections 3, 9, and 11 and Example 5.7.
   The associator [x, y, z] of (A, ◦) is the trilinear map ⊗3 A∗ → A defined by
(1.5)                   [x, y, z] = (x ◦ y) ◦ z − x ◦ (y ◦ z) = (L◦ (x ◦ y) − L◦ (x)L◦ (y)) z.
An analogy, not developed in full detail here, treating the structure tensor of the multiplication as parallel to
an affine connection, leads to regarding the associator as an analogue of the curvature tensor of a connection.
Following this analogy, the Ricci form of a commutative algebra (A, ◦) is defined to be the bilinear trace-form
(1.6)                 ρ◦ (x, y) = tr L◦ (x ◦ y) − tr L◦ (x) tr L◦ (y) = tr L◦ (x ◦ y) − τ◦ (x, y).
The expression (1.6) formally mimics the part of the expression for the Ricci curvature that depends al-
gebraically (and not differentially) on the connection coefficients with respect to a background frame (its
Christoffel symbols). Its vanishing is a weak quasi-associativity condition. From this point of view, the in-
variance of a symmetric bilinear form is the algebraic analogue of a symmetric covariant tensor being parallel,
so that the invariance of the Ricci-form is formally analogous to the Einstein equations for a Riemannian
metric (which imply that its Ricci form is parallel). For an exact commutative algebra the Ricci form is
the negative of the Killing form, ρ◦ = −τ◦ , and this line of thinking provides an independent motivation for
studying Killing metrized algebras. The most natural generalization of the Killing metrized condition to the
context of not necessarily exact commutative algebras is Ricci metrizability. A commutative algebra is Ricci
invariant if its Ricci form is invariant, and Ricci metrized if its Ricci form is invariant and nondegenerate.
   An associative Killing metrized commutative algebra is necessarily unital (it follows from Lemma 2.4 that
a Killing metrized commutative algebra with vanishing Ricci form is necessarily unital). A unital, associative
k-algebra (A, ◦) is split if it is isomorphic to a product of n copies of k. A unital, associative k-algebra (A, ◦)
is étale if A ⊗k l is split for some field extension l of k. An étale algebra is necessarily commutative. An
n-dimensional associative commutative k-algebra is Killing metrized if and only if it is an étale k-algebra of
rank n [13, Proposition 1, A.V.47]. By the Artin-Wedderburn theorem, the algebra is Killing metrizable if
and only if (A, ◦) is a direct sum of finite separable field extensions of k whose ranks sum to n. In particular,
over an algebraically closed field k, an étale k-algebra is necessarily split. The operative assumptions in this
setting can be relaxed in various senses. The first is to consider not algebraically closed fields; this amounts
to considering the general setting of étale algebras, where the complications are codified in Galois theory and
its extensions. The second is to relax the associativity (and the concomitant unitality condition - unitality
and associativity are linked in general [69]) while maintaining the commutativity and the invariance and
nondegeneracy of the trace-form. This provides still another motivation for considering Killing metrized
commutative algebras.
   The most basic examples of Killing metrized exact commutative algebras are the simplicial algebras En (k),
a family of commutative nonassociative algebras, one for each dimension n ≥ 2, first studied in [22] and [43]
as models of an algebraic structure with automorphisms equal to the symmetric group. The simplicial
algebras are the only exact algebras in a one-parameter family of Ricci metrized algebras Snα (k) described
4                                               DANIEL J. F. FOX


in Section 3. The family Snα (k) plays an important role in the classification of cyclic elements in semisimple
Lie algebras [23].
   The analogy between the associator of a metrized commutative algebra and the curvature tensor of a
metric leads to a notion called here sectional nonassociativity directly modeled on the usual notion of sectional
curvature. It facilitates quantifications of nonassociativity that in particular generalize the Norton inequality
that plays an important role in the study of Griess algebras [44, Chapter 8]. The Norton inequality is the
same as nonnegative sectional nonassociativity. For metrized commutative algebras, the corresponding upper
bound, nonpositive sectional nonassociativity, has consequences reminiscent of conclusions stemming from
nonpositive sectional curvature, for example finiteness of the automorphism group (see Lemma 8.9). However,
the notion of sectional nonassociativity facilitates quantifications more precise than positivity or negativity,
namely sharper bounds, or constancy. In particular, it is shown that constant sectional nonassociativity is
related to notions of vanishing projective or conformal nonassociativity. For example, Theorem 6.8 gives a
new characterization of the simplicial algebras in terms of sectional associativity; precisely, for k algebraically
closed or real, an projectively associative exact commutative algebra with nondegenerate Killing form is
isomorphic to a simplicial algebra.
   Following the analogy with the curvature of a metric connection, a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h)
is Killing Einstein if there is 0 6= κ ∈ k such that τ◦ = κh. The element κ is called the Einstein constant.
In this case (A, ◦) is Killing metrized, there is no need to mention h in the definition. The inclusion of h
in the definition reflects an analogy with the Einstein equations for a Riemannian metric. These equations
state that the Ricci curvature of the Levi-Civita connection of a Riemannian metric is a multiple of the
metric. At least in the case of nonzero scalar curvature, they could equally well be formulated as the Ricci
curvature of an affine connection is nondegenerate and parallel. The Einstein constant κ is the analogue of
the scalar curvature. The Killing Einstein condition is considered here only for exact commutative algebras.
The more appropriate notion for not necessarily exact metrized commutative algebras is that such an algebra
is Einstein if its Ricci-form is a nonzero multiple of h. Note that the Ricci form of a unital commutative
algebra is necessarily degenerate for the unit e satisfies ρ◦ (e, · ) = 0.
   This perspective is most appropriate when some natural invariant metric h is fixed in advance and some
work is needed to show that τ◦ is a multiple of h. For example, such a situation occurs in Examples 5.7
and 8.5, where it is explained that results of Matsuo and Miyamoto yield that the deunitalizations of the
Griess algebras of certain vertex operator algebras are Killing metrized exact commutative algebras with
nonnegative sectional nonassociativity. In this context there is a natural invariant metric h induced directly
from the vertex operator algebra structure, and it is interesting to calculate the Einstein constant κ such
that τ◦ = κh. This is vaguely analogous to the much simpler example of a simple matrix Lie algebra, for
which the Killing form is a constant multiple of the Frobenius trace form on the matrix algebra.
   A variety of examples other than the Snα (k) are described. These include tensor products of simplicial
algebras, the deunitalizations of simple Euclidean Jordan algebras (trace-free Hermitian matrices over real
Hurwitz algebras, equipped with the trace-free Jordan product), the tensor products of simple Lie algebras,
and the Griess algebras of OZ vertex operator algebras. Sections 4, 9, and 11 and Example 5.7 describe
these applications of the general theory to specific examples. They are largely independent and can be read
separately.
   Section 4 describes partial results about the structure of the tensor products of simplicial algebras with
themselves. Although En (k) is simple for all n ≥ 2, the tensor product Em (k) ⊗k En (k) is not simple if
m = 2 or m ≥ 6, although it is if m = 3. A peculiar and particular role is played by the tensor product with
E3 (k) for the following reason. By (1.2) the cubic form determined by polarizing the cubic polynomial of a
metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) can be regarded as the cubic polynomial of a metrized commutative
algebra structure on the threefold sum A ⊕ A ⊕ A. The triple algebra construction is a special case of a
more general construction described in Section 11 and associating to a not necessarily commutative algebra
a metrized exact commutative algebra, that when applied to semisimple Lie algebras recovers the Nahm
algebras studied by M. Kinyon and A. Sagle [48]. Lemma 11.3 shows that the triple algebra of a metrized
commutative algebra is isomorphic to its tensor product with E3 (k) while the Nahm algebra of semisimple
Lie algebra is isomorphic to its tensor product with so(3). This formal parallelism between E3 (k) and so(3)
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                  5


is partly explained in [44], where Ivanov has considered algebras satisfying conditions related to those here.
(More generally, the point of view taken here has a lot in common with that advocated in [44], where the
Killing trace-form and Norton inequality explicitly play important roles.)
   The author believes that the simplicial algebras should play a role in the general theory somewhat akin
to that played by Cartan subalgebras in Lie theory. In particular there should be a notion of rank for
Killing metrized exact commutative algebras, related to the dimension of the maximal subalgebra isomor-
phic to a simplicial algebra. Substance is given to such a speculation by the results of Section 9, where,
among other things, it is shown that the diagonal subalgebra of a deunitalized simple Euclidean Jordan
algebra is a simplicial algebra. That the deunitalizations of simple Euclidean Jordan algebras are Killing
metrized exact algebras follows from results in [66]. Here they are described in order to illustrate the notion
of sectional nonassociativity. Lemma 9.14 shows the simple Euclidean Jordan algebras have nonnegative
sectional nonassociativity and deduces a sharp upper bound on their sectional nonassociativities from the
Chern-do Carmo-Kobayashi-Böttcher-Wenzel inequality (see the appendix). The corresponding bounds on
the sectional nonassociativities of their deunitalizations are given in Corollary 9.19.
   To limit the paper’s size, two of the most interesting applications are omitted, except for some remarks
in Section 12:
      • The algebras of metric Weyl and Kähler Weyl curvature tensors studied in [25] are Killing metrized
         and exact, so fit into the context considered here.
      • The author has shown [29] that the Norton algebra of the association scheme associated with the
         strongly regular graph [14, 15] determined by a balanced two-distance tight unit norm frame in a
         Euclidean vector space [6, 88] is Killing metrized and exact (for a balanced equiangular tight frame
         this claim follows from [27]). The rich combinatorial structure gives access to explicit information
         about its idempotents and sectional nonassociativity.
   In recent years there has been a lot of activity related to axial algebras [40] and certain algebras associated
with 3-transposition groups and Fischer systems. See [39] for a recent survey of these topics. Although such
algebras often admit an invariant metric (for example see [42, Theorem 4.1]), in general they are not Killing
metrized. However some of them are, and this condition may identify particularly interesting algebras in
these contexts. Examining in detail how axial algebras relate to the algebras considered here seems an
interesting project.
   Most of the ideas presented here, in particular the notion of sectional nonassociativity, extend to a broader
class of not necessarily commutative algebras, that could be called symmetric Frobenius (not necessarily
unital or associative) algebras, that includes also semisimple Lie algebras. A systematic treatment in such
generality is planned for the future.
   In general metrized commutative algebras, bounds on sectional nonassociativity have strong implications
for the spectra of the multiplication endomorphisms of idempotents. These will be studied as part of a joint
project with V. G. Tkachev.
   This paper complements [27], which studies the cubic polynomials of Euclidean Killing metrized exact
commutative algebras without explicitly mentioning the algebraic aspect. A preliminary version of some of
the results reported here is contained in [26, Section 7.2] and reported in [28].

                                       2. Basic structural results
   This section records basic structural results about metrized and Killing metrized commutative algebras
that help to illustrate the importance of the metrized and Killing metrized assumptions. In this section the
base field k is arbitrary except where indicated otherwise.
   An algebra is simple if its multiplication is nontrivial and it has no nontrivial proper ideal. If (A, ◦) is
simple then, since the multiplication is nontrivial, there must hold A ◦ A = A. Note that by this definition a
simple algebra has nonzero dimension.
Lemma 2.1. If I is a simple ideal in a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h), then either I is h-isotropic
or A = I ⊕ I⊥ is a direct sum of h-nondegenerate ideals, where I⊥ = {x ∈ A : h(x, a) = 0 for all a ∈ I} is the
subspace h-orthogonal to I.
6                                                 DANIEL J. F. FOX


Proof. The invariance and nondegeneracy of h implies that a subspace I ⊂ A is an ideal if and only if I⊥
is an ideal. In this case I ∩ I⊥ is an ideal, so if I is simple, then either I ∩ I⊥ = {0}, in which case I is
h-nondegenerate, or I = I⊥ , in which case I is h-isotropic.                                               
Lemma 2.2. If a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) admits a decomposition A = A1 ⊕ A2 as a direct
sum of ideals, then A2 ⊂ (A1 ◦ A1 )⊥ . If, moreover, A1 is simple, then A⊥         1 = A2 and the restriction of h to
A1 is nondegenerate. Consequently, if metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) is expressible as a direct sum
A = ⊕ki=1 Ai of simple ideals Ai , then the summands are nondegenerate and pairwise orthogonal with respect
to h.
                                                                Pk
Proof. Any a1 ∈ A1 ◦ A1 can be written in the form i=1 ri ◦ ti for some ri , ti ∈ A1 . If a2 ∈ A2 , then
              Pk                    Pk
h(a1 , a2 ) = i=1 h(ri ◦ ti , a2 ) = i=1 h(ri , ti ◦ a2 ) = 0, the last equality because ti ◦ a2 ∈ A1 ∩ A2 = {0}. This
shows A2 ⊂ (A1 ◦ A1 )⊥ . If A1 is simple, then it equals A1 ◦ A1 , so A2 ⊂ A⊥   1 . In this case, by the nondegeneracy
of h, dim A⊥                    ⊥                                    ⊥
             1 = dim A2 , so A1 = A2 . Since this means A1 ∩ A1 = A1 ∩ A2 = {0}, the restriction of h to A1 is
nondegenerate.                                                                                                      
   An algebra is semisimple if it is a direct sum of simple ideals. When considering semisimplicity, some care
is necessary, as some equivalences valid for associative algebras fail in its absence, e.g. the characterization
of semisimplicity in terms of the solvable radical is not valid for general algebras. In this regard see [1].
   The multiplication defining an algebra (A, ◦) is faithful if L◦ : A → End(A) is injective.
Lemma 2.3. The multiplication of a metrized semisimple commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) is faithful.
Proof. If L◦ (z) = 0, then, by the invariance of h, 0 = h(L◦ (z)x, y) = h(z, x ◦ y) for all x, y ∈ A, so that
z ∈ (A ◦ A)⊥ . This shows ker L ⊂ (A ◦ A)⊥ . In particular, if A ◦ A = A, then L◦ = (A ◦ A)⊥ = A⊥ = {0}, so
L◦ is injective. If A = ⊕ki=1 Ai isPa direct sumPof simple ideals, then Ai ◦ Aj = {0} if i 6= j, and, since Ai is
simple, Ai ◦ Ai = Ai , so A ◦ A = i Ai ◦ Ai = i Ai = A, and L◦ is injective.                                   
Lemma 2.4. Suppose the Killing metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦) has vanishing Ricci form.
  (1) The unique ℓ ∈ A such that τ◦ (ℓ, x) = tr L◦ (x) for all x ∈ A is a unit.
  (2) A square-zero element z ∈ A is τ◦ -isotropic.
Proof. For x, y ∈ A, τ◦ (ℓ ◦ x, y) = τ◦ (ℓ, x ◦ y) = tr L◦ (x ◦ y) = tr L◦ (x)L◦ (y) = τ◦ (x, y), the first equality
by the invariance of τ◦ and the third equality by the vanishing of the Ricci form. Since this holds for all
y ∈ A, by the nondegeneracy of τ◦ there holds x ◦ ℓ = x for all x ∈ A. If z ∈ A satisfies z ◦ z = 0, then
τ◦ (z, z) = tr L◦ (z)2 = tr L◦ (z ◦ z) = 0.                                                                       
    By Lemma 2.4, an associative Killing metrized commutative algebra is necessarily unital.
Lemma 2.5. Let (A, ◦) be a commutative k-algebra. If there are p, q, r ∈ k such that the symmetric bilinear
form τp,q,r (x, y) = p tr L◦ (x ◦ y) + q tr L◦ (x)L◦ (y) + r tr L◦ (x) tr L◦ (y) is nondegenerate and invariant, then
(A, ◦) is semisimple, expressible, in a unique way up to reordering the factors, as a direct sum A = ⊕i Ai of
simple ideals Ai ⊂ A pairwise orthogonal with respect to τp,q,r .
Proof. Since τ◦ is by assumption invariant, by [73, Theorem 2.6] or [45, Theorem III.5.3] (the argument is due
to Dieudonne [21], where it is given for Lie algebras), the expressibility of A as a direct sum of simple ideals
follows provided B ◦ B 6= {0} for every ideal B 6= {0}. Suppose B ⊂ A is an ideal such that B ◦ B = {0}. For
b ∈ B and any A ∈ End(A) having the form A = IdA +λL◦ (a) for λ ∈ k and a ∈ A, the composition L◦ (b)A
maps A into B and B into {0}, so that (L◦ (b)A)2 = 0. Hence 0 = tr L◦ (b)A = tr L◦ (b) + λ tr L◦ (b)L◦ (a).
Since λ is arbitrary, this implies tr L◦ (b) = 0 and tr L◦ (a)L◦ (b) = 0 for all a ∈ A. Consequently τp,q,r (a, b) =
p tr L◦ (a ◦ b) + q tr L◦ (a)L◦ (b) + r tr L◦ (a) tr L◦ (b) = 0 for all a ∈ A, which by nondegeneracy of τp,q,r implies
b = 0, and hence B = {0}. This suffices to show that (A, ◦) is semisimple. The pairwise orthogonality of the
summands follows from Lemma 2.2.                                                                                     
   Lemma 2.5 implies that a Killing (Ricc) metrized exact commutative algebra is a direct sum of simple
ideals orthogonal with respect to its Killing (Ricci) form. Consequently, to classify Killing (Ricci) metrized
exact commutative algebras, it suffices to classify the simple ones.
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                        7


Lemma 2.6. An n-dimensional associative Killing metrized commutative k-algebra is a split étale algebra
if either k is algebraically closed or k = R and its Killing form is positive definite.
Proof. In either case, by Lemma 2.4, (A, ◦) is unital. By Lemma 2.5 it is a direct sum of τ◦ -orthogonal simple
ideals. By the Artin-Wedderburn theorem each of these simple ideals is isomorphic to a matrix algebra over
a division ring over k, and by the commutativity of ◦, each of these must be a commutative division ring
over k, so a field extension of k. If k is algebraically closed the conclusion follows, while if k = R and τ◦ is
positive definite, then each of the simple ideals is isomorphic to R or C, and since the restriction of τ◦ to
each ideal is an invariant metric with definite signature, each must be isomorphic to R.                      
Example 2.7. The conclusion of Lemma 2.5 is false for general metrized commutative algebras as the
following simple example illustrates. Let char k = 0 and for ǫ ∈ k consider the commutative, associative,
unital algebra A = k[t]/(t2 − ǫ). For λ ∈ A∗ defined by λ(x0 + x1 t) = x1 , the metric h(x, y) = λ(xy) =
x0 y1 + x1 y0 is nondegenerate and invariant. The Killing form τ◦ (x, y) = x0 y0 + ǫx1 y1 is invariant and is
nondegenerate provided ǫ 6= 0. When ǫ = 0, A is not semisimple, for the ideal generated by t is isotropic and
squares to zero and the Killing form τ◦ (x, y) = 2x1 y1 is degenerate. When ǫ 6= 0, the metric h is insensitive
to ǫ, while the metric τ◦ is sensitive to ǫ. The properties of the metrized algebra (A, τ ) depend on ǫ if k is
not algebraically closed. For example, in the case k = R, when ǫ < 0, then A is isomorphic to the complex
numbers, viewed as a R-algebra, and the Killing form has split signature, while when ǫ > 0, A is isomorphic
to the paracomplex numbers, C viewed as a real vector space with the multiplication u ◦ v = ūv̄, and the
Killing form is positive definite.
   This example illustrates two different aspects of the conditions imposed on the Killing form. The nonde-
generacy forces semisimplicity of the algebra. When the base field is not algebraically closed the structure
of a metrized algebra depends on more refined properties of the metric as a bilinear form, for example, in
the real case, whether it is or is not positive definite. For example, C is metrizable as a R-algebra, for
hu, vi = uv + ūv̄ is a split signature invariant R-valued metric, but C admits no definite signature invariant
metric h, for an invariant metric h must satisfy −h(1, 1) = h(1, i ◦ i) = h(i, i). This shows the necessity of
the assumption of definite signature in Lemma 2.6 for its conclusion in the case k = R.
   With anisotropy of τ◦ in place of positive definiteness, the conclusion of Lemma 2.6 is false over more
general fields. A simple example of an étale algebra that is not split is the degree 3 cyclic Galois extension
of Q obtained by adjoining a root of r3 + r2 − 2r − 1, which has positive definite Killing form and is not split
over Q (if it were, it would admit three orthogonal idempotents, but in a field any idempotent equals 1). ⊳
  The tensor product A1 ⊗k A2 of commutative or anticommutative k-algebras (A1 , ◦1 ) and (A2 , ◦2 ) is a
commutative algebra with the product ◦ defined on decomposable elements by
(2.1)                              (a1 ⊗ b1 ) ◦ (a2 ⊗ b2 ) = (a1 ◦1 a2 ) ⊗ (b1 ◦2 b2 ),
and extending linearly. Alternatively, L◦ (a1 ⊗ a2 ) = L◦1 (a1 ) ⊗ L◦2 (a2 ). Since decomposable elements
span A1 ⊗k A2 and tr L◦ (a1 ⊗ a2 ) = tr L◦1 (a1 ) ⊗ L◦2 (a2 ), (A1 ⊗k A2 , ◦) is exact if either of (A1 , ◦1 ) or
(A2 , ◦2 ) is exact. If h1 and h2 are invariant metrics on (A1 , ◦1 ) and (A2 , ◦2 ), then h1 ⊗ h2 defined by
(h1 ⊗ h2 )(a1 ⊗ b1 , a2 ⊗ b2 ) = h1 (a1 , a2 )h2 (b1 , b2 ) is invariant on (A1 ⊗k A2 , ◦), for
            (h1 ⊗ h2 )((a1 ⊗ b1 ) ◦ (a2 ⊗ b2 ), a3 ⊗ b3 ) = h1 (a1 ◦1 a2 , a3 )h2 (b1 ◦1 b2 , b3 )
(2.2)
                        = h1 (a1 , a2 ◦1 a3 )h2 (b1 , b2 ◦2 b3 ) = (h1 ⊗ h2 )(a1 ⊗ b1 , (a2 ⊗ b2 ) ◦ (a3 ⊗ b3 )).
Define the tensor product of metrized commutative algebras (A1 , ◦1 , h1 ) and (A2 , ◦2 , h2 ) to be (A1 ⊗k A2 , ◦, h1 ⊗
h2 ).
Lemma 2.8.
  (1) The tensor product of metrized commutative k-algebras (A1 , ◦1 , h1 ) and (A2 , ◦2 , h2 ) at least one of
      which is exact is a metrized exact commutative k-algebra.
  (2) The tensor product of Killing metrized commutative k-algebras (A1 , ◦1 ) and (A2 , ◦2 ) at least one of
      which is exact is a Killing metrized exact commutative k-algebra.
  (3) The tensor product (g ⊗k h, ◦) of semisimple Lie algebras g and h over a field k of characteristic zero
      is a Killing Einstein exact commutative algebra.
8                                                    DANIEL J. F. FOX


Proof. If {a1 , . . . , adim A1 } and {b1 , . . . , bm2 } are bases of A1 and A2 , then {ai ⊗ bj : 1 ≤ i ≤ m1 , 1 ≤ j ≤ m2 }
is a basis of A1 ⊗k A2 , and the Gram matrix of h1 ⊗ h2 with respect to this basis is the tensor product of
the Gram matrices of h1 and h2 , which are invertible because h1 and h2 are nondegenerate, so the tensor
product (A1 ⊗k A2 , ◦, h1 ⊗ h2 ) is a metrized commutative algebra. If (A1 , ◦1 ) and (A2 , ◦2 ) are either both
commutative or both anticommutative, the Killing form of (A1 ⊗k A2 , ◦) equals τ◦1 ⊗ τ◦2 , for
              τ◦ (a1 ⊗ b1 , a2 ⊗ b2 ) = tr L◦ (a1 ⊗ b1 )L◦ (a2 ⊗ b2 )
(2.3)
                                     = tr (L◦1 (a1 )L◦1 (a2 ) ⊗ L◦2 (b1 )L◦2 (b2 )) = τ◦1 (a1 , a2 )τ◦2 (b1 , b2 ).
By (2.3), if τ◦1 and τ◦2 are invariant or nondegenerate, then τ◦ has the same property. This shows that
the tensor product of Killing metrized algebras qua commutative algebras equals their tensor product qua
metrized algebras. Similarly, if A1 = g and A2 = h are semisimple Lie algebras over a field of characteristic
zero, then their Killing forms are nondegenerate and invariant, so their tensor product is a Killing metrized
commutative algebra that is exact because a semisimple Lie algebra is unimodular.                          
   (Essentially the same arguments show the analogous statements are true for Ricci metrized commutative
algebras.)
Remark 2.9. The special case of the tensor product of Lie algebras so(3) ⊗ g is isomorphic to what was
called the Nahm algebra of g in [48]. See Section 11 for further discussion of this example.             ⊳
Example 2.10. For semisimple Lie algebras g and h, Aut(g ⊗k h) contains Aut(g) × Aut(h) acting on each
factor separately. This shows that the automorphism group of a Killing metrized exact commutative algebra
can be large.                                                                                            ⊳
   In any class of algebras general structural properties are reflected in the behavior of idempotents and
square-zero elements. An element e ∈ A is idempotent if e ◦ e = e and square-zero if e ◦ e = 0. Let
I(A, ◦) = {0 6= e ∈ A : e ◦ e = e} and N(A, ◦) = {0 6= z ∈ A : z ◦ z = 0}.
Lemma 2.11. If the metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) is expressible as a direct sum ⊕ki=1 Ai of h-
orthogonal ideals Ai ⊂ A and πi : A → Ai is the h-orthogonal projection onto the ith component, then e ∈ A
is idempotent if and only if its projections ei = πi (e) are idempotents.
                                                               Pk
Proof. Because ei ◦ ej = 0 if i 6= j and ei ◦ ei ∈ Ai , e ◦ e = i=1 ei ◦ ei and so πi (e ◦ e) = ei ◦ ei , from which
the claim follows.                                                                                                
   For λ ∈ k, let A(λ) (e) = {x ∈ A : L◦ (e)x = λx} and define the spectrum of e ∈ A by Π(e) = {λ ∈ k :
dim A(λ) (e) > 0}. For a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h), the endomorphism L◦ (e) is h-self-adjoint
for any e ∈ A. If e ∈ I(A, ◦), then L◦ (e) preserves hei⊥ = {y ∈ A : h(e, y) = 0}, for if h(e, y) = 0 then
h(L◦ (e)y, e) = h(y, e ◦ e) = h(y, e) = 0. For an h-anisotropic idempotent e, define the orthogonal spectrum
(2.4)                      Π⊥ (e) = {λ ∈ k : there is x ∈ hei⊥ such that L◦ (e)x = λx},
so that Π(e) = Π⊥ (e) ∪ {1}.
   Idempotents e and f in a commutative algebra are orthogonal if e ◦ f = 0. In a metrized commutative
algebra (A, ◦, h), orthogonal idempotents e and f are h-orthogonal, for h(e, f ) = h(e ◦ e, f ) = h(e, e ◦ f ) = 0,
by the invariance of h. When char k 6= 2, if an idempotent e can be written as a sum of nonzero idempotents
u and v, then necessarily u and v are orthogonal, for in this case u + v = e = e ◦ e = u + v + 2u ◦ v.
   A nonzero idempotent idempotent is primitive if it cannot be written as a sum of two nonzero idempo-
tents. Following [41], a nonzero idempotent e in a metrized commutative algebra is absolutely primitive if
dim A(1) (e) = 1; that is 1 has multiplicity 1 in Π(e).
Lemma 2.12. Suppose char k 6= 2. In a metrized commutative k-algebra (A, ◦, h), an absolutely primitive
anisotropic idempotent is primitive.
Proof. Suppose the absolutely primitive anisotropic idempotent e equals u+v where u and v are idempotents,
necessarily orthogonal. Write u = αe + x and v = βe + y where α, β ∈ k and x and y are h-orthogonal to e.
Because h(e, e) 6= 0, x and y are not multiples of e. That e = u + v = (α + β)e + x + y forces β = 1 − α and
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                     9


y = −x. That u and v be idempotents implies the equations αe + x = α2 + 2αe ◦ x + x ◦ x and (1 − α)e − x =
(1 − α)2 e + 2(α − 1)e ◦ x + x ◦ x. The difference of these two equations yields (2α − 1)e + 2x = (2α − 1)e + 2e ◦ x,
so L◦ (e)x = x, that is x ∈ A(1) (e). Since h(x, e) = 0 and h(e, e) 6= 0, this forces x = 0. It follows that u and
v are multiples of e, and since they are orthogonal, one of them must be 0.                                       
   A metrized commutative R-algebra is Euclidean if the metric is positive definite. Such an algebra is called
a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra; the omitted qualifier real is implied by the use of Euclidean. The
Euclidean assumption has strong implications for the properties of idempotents.
   An idempotent e in a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra, (A, ◦, h) is minimal if there is no f ∈ I(A, ◦)
such that |f |h < |e|h . By definition two minimal idempotents have the same h-norm. Given a metrized
commutative algebra (A, ◦, h), let M(A, ◦, h) ⊂ I(A, ◦) be the set (in principal possibly empty) of minimal
idempotents in (A, ◦, h). The notion of minimal idempotent depends only on the positive homothety class
of h and not on h itself. Minimal idempotents play an important role in the structure theory for Euclidean
metrized commutative algebras. Similar considerations are important in the study of Griess algebras, going
back at least to [64]. V. G. Tkachev and Y. Krasnov and collaborators have studied idempotents extensively
in metrized algebras [51, 52, 66, 80, 82, 83, 84]. Lemma 2.13 summarizes some basic facts that are needed
here.
Lemma 2.13 ([81, Proposition 2.5], [82, Lemma 2.3], [83, Proposition 2.1]).
  (1) Let (A, ◦, h) be a metrized commutative R-algebra with cubic polynomial P . An anisotropic e ∈ A
      is a critical point of the restriction of P to Sh(e,e) if and only if there holds one of the following
      conditions.
       (a) P (e) 6= 0 and h(e,e)
                           6P (e) e is idempotent in A.
       (b) P (e) = 0 and e ◦ e = 0.
  (2) Let (A, ◦, h) be a nontrivial Euclidean metrized commutative algebra.
       (a) M(A, ◦, h) is nonempty and any two minimal idempotents have the same h-norm.
       (b) e ∈ M(A, ◦, h) is primitive and absolutely primitive and Π⊥ (e) ⊂ (−∞, 1/2].

                                            3. Simplicial algebras
   This section treats a one-parameter family of algebras Snα (k) that play a basic role.
Definition 3.1. Let k be a field of characteristic zero. For α ∈ k, let (Snα (k), ◦) be the n-dimensional
commutative algebra generated by {ei : 1 ≤ i ≤ n} subject to the relations
(3.1)               ei ◦ ei = ei ,                   ei ◦ ej = α(ei + ej ),   i 6= j ∈ {1, . . . , n}.
   For α = −1/(n − 1) these algebras were studied by Griess and Harada as algebras with automorphism
group Sn+1 . The full family plays a role in the work of Elashvili-Jibladze-Kac [23] classifying cyclic elements
in semisimple Lie algebras. Here these algebras are basic examples in relation to the notions of projective
and conformal associativity and sectional nonassociativity introduced later.

Remark 3.12. R. L. Griess, in unpublished work in 1977 (see the second paragraph of [74] and [22, appen-

Lemma 3.13. Let k be a field of characteristic zero and let α ∈ k \ {0, 1/2}. Let {e1 , . . . , en } be the
idempotents generating Snα (k) and satisfying (3.1). For I = {i1 , . . . , ip } ⊂ {1, . . . , n}, define eI = ei1 + · · · +

Corollary 3.17 (R. Griess [22, appendix], K. Harada [43]). The automorphism group of En (k) is the
symmetric group Sn+1 .
Proof. Since Aut(En ) permutes the idempotents of En (k) and preserves τ◦ , it permutes the set of absolutely
primitive idempotents, {γ0 , . . . , γn }, so acts as a subgroup of Sn+1 . Because an element of Aut(En (k)) which
fixes n of the idempotents {γ0 , . . . , γn } must act on En (k) as the identity, the group Aut(En (k)) is equal to
Sn+1 .                                                                                                           

Lemma 3.19. Let {γ0 , . . . , γn } be the spanning set in En (k) as in 3.10.
     (1) Φ = {rij = γi − γj : 0 ≤ i 6= j ≤ n} is a root system of type An .
     (2) For 0 ≤ i ≤ n − 1, the reflection σij through the hyperplane τ◦ -orthogonal to the element rij is an
             automorphism of En (k) that acts on {γ0 , . . . , γn } as the transposition interchanging γi and γj and
                             / {i, j}.The automorphism group Aut(En (k)) is identified with Sn+1 acting irreducibly
             fixing γk for k ∈
             as the group generated by these reflections.
                        P
Proof. As nγi = 0≤k≤n,k6=i rik , the rij span En (k). The only scalar multiple of rij in Φ is rji = −rij .
Using i, j, k, l to denote pairwise distinct indices, it follows from (3.4) that τ◦ (rij , rij ) = 2(n + 1)/(n − 1),
τ◦ (rij , rik ) = (n + 1)/(n − 1), and τ◦ (rij , rkl ) = 0. Hence the pairing hx, yi = 2τ◦ (x, y)/τ◦ (x, x) is integral on
Φ. The reflection σij (x) = x − hrij , xirij through the hyperplane τ◦ -orthogonal to rij acts on {γ0 , . . . , γn }
as the transposition interchanging γi and γj and fixing γk for k ∈        / {i, j}, and hence preserves Φ. This shows
that Φ is a root system. Write ri = ri i+1 . Then ∆ = {ri : 0 ≤ i ≤ n − 1} is a set of simple roots and
the reflections σi through the hyperplanes τ◦ -orthogonal to the ri ∈ ∆ suffice to generate an action of Sn+1 .
As hri , rj i is −1 if |i − j| = 1 and 0 if |i − j| > 1, Φ has type An . It is straightforward to check that, for
the reflection σ associated to any element of Φ, σ(γi ) ◦ σ(γj ) = σ(γi ◦ γj ) for all 0 ≤ i, j ≤ n. Since the
γi span En (k) and the σij generate Sn+1 , it follows that Sn+1 acts on En (k) by automorphisms permuting
{γ0 , . . . , γn }. Since Aut(En (k)) acts faithfully on this set, this shows Aut(En (k)) = Sn+1 .                       

                               4. Tensor products of simplicial algebras
   This section describes some results about the structure of E2 (k) ⊗ En (k). It would be interesting to
describe Em (k) ⊗ En (k) in general. The structure of E2 (k) ⊗ En (k) depends more strongly on n than might
be suspected. Lemma 4.2 shows that E2 (k) ⊗ E2 (k) is isomorphic to E2 (k) ⊕ E2 (k) and Lemma 4.4 shows
that E2 (k) ⊗ En (k) is not simple if n ≥ 6, while Theorem 11.19 shows that, for any n ≥ 2, E3 (k) ⊗ En (k) is
simple and is not isomorphic to E3n (k).
   Let {ei : 0 ≤ i ≤ 2} be the minimal idempotents in E2 (k) and let {eα : 0 ≤ α ≤ n} be the minimal
idempotents in En (k). Then {eiα = ei ⊗ eα : 0 ≤ i ≤ 2, 0 ≤ α ≤ n} are idempotents in E2 (k) ⊗ En (k) that

Lemma 4.1. If char k = 0, E2 (k) ⊗ E5 (k) is not isomorphic to E10 (k).
Proof. Equation (4.8) shows that E2 (k) ⊗ E5 (k) contains a nontrivial square-zero element, while Lemma 3.16
shows that E10 (k) contains no nontrivial square-zero element.                                            

Lemma 4.2. If char k = 0, E2 (k) ⊗ E2 (k) is isomorphic to E2 (k) ⊕ E2 (k).

product of an element of {a012 , a120 , a201 } an element of {a021 , a210 , a102 } is 0, so these generate orthogonal
ideals in E2 ⊗ E2 each isomorphic to E2 .                                                                          

                                    5. Unitalization and deunitalization
    The classical Thomas construction associates with a projective connection on a manifold M a Ricci flat
affine connection on a one-dimensional fiber bundle over M such that the lifted connection is flat if and
only if the original projection connection is projectively flat. This is an extension to a curved differential
geometric setting of the usual processes of homogenization and projectivization. The formally analogous
notions for commutative algebras are the unitalization and deunitalization constructions described in this
section. These are used in Section 6 to show that certain quasi-associativity conditions modeled on notions of
projective and conformal flatness for connections on vector bundles can be used to characterize the simplicial
algebras over R or an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.
    For a symmetric bilinear form c ∈ S 2 A∗ , the c-unitalization of the commutative algebra (A, ◦) is Â = A ⊕ k
equipped with the commutative multiplication ˆ◦ defined by
(5.1)                                  ◦(y, β) = (x ◦ y + αy + βx, αβ + c(x, y)),
                                 (x, α)ˆ
                              2 ∗
and the bilinear form ĉ ∈ S Â defined by ĉ((x, λ), (y, µ)) = c(x, y) + λµ. By definition (0, 1) ∈ Â is a unit,
so (Â, ˆ◦) is a unital commutative algebra. That ĉ is ˆ◦-invariant if and only if c is ◦-invariant follows from the
observation that
(5.2)                       ◦(y, β), (z, γ)) = c(x ◦ y, z) + αc(y, z) + βc(x, z) + γc(x, y) + αβγ
                   ĉ((x, α)ˆ
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                     17


is completely symmetric if and only if c is ◦-invariant.
   The unitalization of a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) is its h-unitalization, (Â, ˆ◦, ĥ), which is a
unital metrized commutative algebra.
Remark 5.1. The usual definition of the unitalization of an algebra is what is called here the c-unitalization
with c identically zero. The unitalization as defined here is the unitalization within a category whose objects
are metrized commutative algebras.                                                                              ⊳
   Lemma 5.3 shows that two metrized commutative algebras are isomorphic if and only if their unitalizations
are isometrically isomorphic.
   A homomorphism of metrized commutative algebras is an algebra homomorphism Ψ : (A, ◦, h) → (B, ¯◦, g)
that is metric-preserving in the sense that Ψ∗ (g) = h. By the nondegeneracy of h and g, Ψ is injective. An
isomorphism of metrized commutative algebras is a bijective homomorphism of such algebras whose inverse is
also a homomorphism of metrized commutative algebras; equivalently it is an isometric algebra isomorphism.
The group Aut(A, ◦, h) of automorphisms of (A, ◦, h) is Aut(A, ◦, h) = Aut(A, ◦) ∩ O(h).
   In the context of nonunital algebras, a homomorphism of algebras that happen to be unital is not required
to preserve units.
Lemma 5.2. Let Φ : (A, ◦) → (B, ¯ ◦) be a homomorphism of metrized commutative algebras and suppose
e ∈ A and f ∈ B are units. Then Φ(e) = f if f ∈ im Φ.
Proof. If f = Φ(u), then Φ(x) = f ¯ ◦Φ(x) = Φ(u)¯◦Φ(x) = Φ(u ◦ x) for all x ∈ A. Because Φ is injective, this
implies u is a unit in A, so must equal e, because a unit is unique if it exists.                          

Lemma 5.3. Let (Â, ˆ    ◦, ĥ) and (B̂, b̄◦, ĝ) be the unitalizations of the metrized commutative algebras (A, ◦, h)
and (B, ¯◦, g).
                           ◦, ĥ) → (B̂, b̄
    (1) The lift Ψ̂ : (Â, ˆ             ◦, ĝ), defined by Ψ̂(x, α) = (Ψ(x), α), is a homomorphism of metrized com-

If g is anisotropic, that g(u, u) = 0 implies u = 0. Since Φ(x, 0) = (Ψ(x), 0), if Φ is surjective, then Ψ is
surjective and that u is g-orthgonal to Ψ(A) = B implies u = 0. If (0, 1) = Φ(0, 1) = (u, 1), then u = 0. In
any of these cases, (5.6) implies Ψ is a homomorphism of metrized commutative algebras. Because the lift
of a composition of homomorphisms is the composition of their lifts, claim (3) follows from (1) and (2). 
   Let (B, ⋆, g) be a metrized commutative algebra and let A ⊂ B be a g-nondegenerate subspace. The
g-orthogonal projection π of B on A means the projection onto A along the transverse subspace A⊥ = {x ∈
B : g(x, y) = 0 for all y ∈ A}. The multiplication ◦ defined on B by x ◦ y = π(π(x) ⋆ π(y)) satisfies
           g(x ◦ y, z) = g(π(π(x) ⋆ π(y)), z) = g(π(π(x) ⋆ π(y)), π(z)) = g(π(x) ⋆ π(y), π(z))
(5.7)
               = g(π(x), π(y) ⋆ π(z)) = g(π(x), π(π(y) ⋆ π(z))) = g(x, π(π(y) ⋆ π(z))) = g(x, y ◦ z),
so (B, ◦, g) is also a metrized commutative algebra. As B◦ B ⊂ A, A is a two-sided ideal of (B, ◦, g). Modifying
slightly terminology from [37], the algebra (A, ◦, g) is called the retraction of (B, ⋆, g) onto the g-nondegenerate
subspace A.
    The deunitalization (A, ◦, h) of a metrized unital commutative algebra (B, ⋆, g) having a nonistropic unit
e is the retraction of (B, ⋆, h = g(e, e)−1 g) along e (note that the induced metric is h, not g). The terminol-
ogy deunitalization is justified by Lemma 5.4 that shows that unitalization and deunitalization are inverse
operations in a precise sense. The factor of g(e, e)−1 in the definition of the metric h of a deunitalization is
necessary for the first claim of Lemma 5.4 to hold.
Lemma 5.4. A unital metrized commutative algebra (B, ⋆, g) with anisotropic unit is isomorphic to the
                   ◦, ĥ) of its deunitalization (A, ◦, h) via the map Π : B → Â defined by Π(x + αe) = (x, α) ∈ Â.
unitalization (Â, ˆ
A metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) is isomorphic to the deunitalization of its unitalization (Â, ˆ◦, ĥ) via
the inclusion i : A → Â defined by i(x) = (x, 0).
Proof. From the definition of ⋆ it follows that
(5.8)                      (x + αe) ⋆ (y + βe) = x ◦ y + αy + βx + (h(x, y) + αβ) e,
for x, y ∈ A and α, β ∈ k. Comparing (5.8) with (5.1) yields the first claim. By definition the deunitalization
of (Â, ˆ◦, ĥ) is the vector space i(A) equipped with the multiplication ⋆ defined by i(x) ⋆ i(y) = i(x)ˆ   ◦i(y) −
ĥ(i(x), i(y))(0, 1) = (x, 0)ˆ ◦(y, 0) − (0, h(x, y)) = (x ◦ y, 0) = i(x ◦ y), and the metric obtained by restricting
ĥ. This shows second claim.                                                                                       
Lemma 5.5. Let (B, ⋆, g) be a metrized unital commutative algebra with anisotropic unit e and suppose there
are constants A and B such that
(5.9)                               τ(B,⋆) (x, y) = Ag(x, e)g(y, e) + Bg(x, y),
for x, y ∈ B. Then dim B = Ag(e, e)2 + Bg(e, e), and the deunitalization (A, ◦, h) is exact and satisfies
τ(A,◦) = κh with κ = Bg(e, e) − 2.
Proof. By (6.2) combined with Lemma 5.3, if x ∈ A, then tr L(B,⋆) (x) = tr L◦ (x). Since g(x, e) = 0 if x ∈ A,
taking x ∈ A and y = e in (5.9) gives tr L◦ (x) = tr L(B,⋆) (x) = (Ag(e, e) + B)g(x, e) = 0. This shows that the
deunitalization (A, ◦, h) is exact. Taking x = e = y in (5.9) gives dim B = tr L(B,⋆) (e) = Ag(e, e)2 + Bg(e, e).
Combined with Lemma 5.3, (6.3) yields that τ(B,⋆) (x, y) = τ(A,◦) (x, y) + 2h(x, y), for x, y ∈ A, and with (5.9)
this yields τ(A,◦) (x, y) = (Bg(e, e) − 2)h(x, y).                                                             
   Lemma 5.5 can be used to check that the deunitalizations of certain algebras are Killing metrized.
Example 5.6. In [36], R. L. Griess constructed a 196883-dimensional metrized commutative algebra (A, ⋆, h),
called here the 196883-dimensional (nonunital) Griess algebra, and constructed a finite simple group M of
monster type acting on (A, ⋆, h) as automorphisms. In Griess’s construction of (A, ⋆, h) it is defined over Q;
here it is considered over R. For the construction of the monster finite simple group and the associated Griess
                 COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                 19


algebra consult [31] or [44]. It was shown by J. Tits in [78] that M is the automorphism group of (A, ⋆, h).
In [77] and [38] it was established that the group M is unique up to isomorphism, justifying calling it the
monster finite simple group. It is known that M has a unique complex simple representation of dimension
196883, from which it follows that h is uniquely determined up to homothety. The final equation on page
498 of [78] establishes that τ◦ is a nonzero multiple of h (called there a “curious relation”; this also appears
as equation (2) of section 5 of [79], although the normalizations in [78] and [79] are different).
   In studying M it is common to work with some 196844-dimensional unital metrized commutative algebra
the deunitalization of which is the 196883-dimensional Griess algebra (A, ⋆, h), and different references, e.g.
[36, 37, 19, 31], use slightly different extensions (any such algebra is commonly also called a Griess algebra).
Here the 196884-dimensional extension (B, ◦, g) is taken as in [19], except that g is scaled so that the unit
e has g-norm 1 (in [19], the unit has length 3/2). This algebra (so scaled) is called here the Conway-
Griess algebra. By the formula at the end of section 13 of [19], there holds (5.9) with A = 183024 and
B = 13860. Substituted into (5.9) of Lemma 5.5 this shows that the Killing form of the Griess algebra
satisfies τ◦ = (4 · 132 · 41)h = 13858h, which agrees with the cited result of [78] up to normalizations.
   As is explained in Example 8.4, the Griess algebra satisfies Norton’s inequality, so has nonnegative sectional
nonassociativity in the sense of Section 7.                                                                     ⊳
Example 5.7. Calculations of Matsuo [62] can be used to show the deunitalizations of Griess algebras of
certain vertex operator algebras are Killing metrized exact commutative algebras and to calculate their
Einstein constants with respect to a reference metric induced from the vertex operator algebra structure.
This entails no new results about such algebras and amounts to translating known results into the language
and context used here, so should be understood as an extended example.
   A vertex operator algebra (VOA) over a field k is an k-vector space V equipped with a linear map Y : V →
(End V)[[z, z −1 ]], called the state-field correspondence and written
                                                X
(5.10)                               Y (a, z) =     a(n) z −n−1 ∈ (End V)[[z, z −1 ]],

taking values in endomorphism-valued formal power series, and two distinguished vectors, the vacuum vector

that together satisfy the axioms:
    (1) (locality) For a, b ∈ V, there is N > 0 such that a(n) b = 0 if n ≥ N .
    (2) Y (1, z) = IdV .
    (3) For a ∈ V, Y (a, z)1 ∈ V[[z]] and limn→0 Y (a, z) = a.
    (4) (Jacobi/Borcherds identity) For a, b ∈ V and l, m, n ∈ Z,

These axioms force 1 ∈ V0 and Vi(n) Vj ⊂ Vi+j−n−1 . (The first four axioms define a vertex algebra; a vertex
algebra satisfying also the last two axioms is a vertex operator algebra.) There are many equivalent definitions
of vertex (operator) algebras. Here the axioms are stated as in [91]; see also [7, 20, 30, 31, 34, 47, 54, 63].
   Here the phrase vertex operator algebra without further qualification means a vertex operator algebra over
C. As for Lie algebras, it is different to speak of a real form of a complex vertex operator algebra and a real
vertex operator algebra. A precise formulation of what is a real form of a VOA is given in [61, section 2].
20                                                   DANIEL J. F. FOX


   A VOA is OZ (short for one-zero) if V0 is generated by 1 and V1 = {0}. For an OZ VOA, the multiplication
⋆ defined by a ⋆ b = a(1) b for a, b ∈ B makes V2 into a commutative algebra and the symmetric bilinear form
g defined by g(a, b)1 = a(3) b for a, b ∈ V2 is invariant with respect to ⋆. This is explained in detail in
[34, section 3.5]. By definition, L(ω) = 2 IdA and g(ω, ω) = c/2. Hence e = ω/2 is a unit in A such that
g(e, e) = c/8. The triple (V2 , ⋆, g) is called the Griess algebra of the OZ VOA. Note that the Griess algebra
of a OZ complex VOA is, by definition, an algebra over C. If the VOA were a real VOA, then its Griess
algebra would be a R-algebra.
Remark 5.8. The terminology differs from that used in example 5.6 in two ways. In example 5.6, the
terminology nonunital Griess algebra refers to a particular algebra, that associated to the Monster Lie
group, whereas here, in the context of VOAs, Griess algebra refers to one of a general class of algebras.
More importantly, in example 5.6 the nonunital Griess algebra is nonunital, while the Griess algebra of an
OZ VOA is by definition unital. The relation between the two uses is however straightforward. There is
constructed in [31] an OZ VOA, the moonshine VOA, whose Griess algebra is a unitalization of the Griess
algebra constructed by Griess. Its Griess algebra is the Conway-Griess algebra of example 5.6.               ⊳
                                                                     n
   In [62, section 1.3] an OZ VOA is defined to be of class S for an integer n ≥ 1 if the action of its
automorphism group on the quotient ⊕nk=0 Vk / ⊕nk=0 Vkω has no fixed point. Here Vω is the submodule of V
generated by the vacuum vector 1 and the action Ln generated by ω, and Vkω = Vω ∩ Vk . By definition, an
OZ VOA of class Sn is of class Sm for all 1 ≤ m < n.

           6. Characterization of simplicial algebras and classification when n ≤ 4
   This section applies the unitalization construction to obtain a characterization of simplicial algebras. It
concludes with the classification of Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative algebras of dimension at
most 4.

Lemma 6.2. Suppose char k = 0. For an n-dimensional commutative k-algebra (A, ◦) with Ricci form ρ◦
the following are equivalent.

      It is convenient to conflate the associator of ◦ with the tensor µ defined by µ(x, y, z) = [y, z, x]. That is,
[x, y, z]l = xi y j z k µkij l for the tensor µijk l = µjk p µpi l − µki p µjp l , where µij k is the structure tensor of the
multiplication ◦. Attention has to be paid to the ordering and position of the indices of µijk l . The algebra
(A, ◦) is associative if and only if µijk l = 0. The Ricci form ρij is obtained as the trace ρij = µpij p and is
symmetric. For a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h), define µijkl = µijk p hpl ∈ ⊗4 A∗ , so that
(6.13)                       µ(a, b, c, d) = h([b, c, a], d) = h(b ◦ c, a ◦ d) − h(c ◦ a, b ◦ d)
for a, b, c, d ∈ A. From the invariance of h with respect to ◦ it follows that µijkl = −µjikl = −µijlk = µklij
and µ[ijk]l = 0, so that µijkl has the symmetries of a metric curvature tensor.
   Define the conformal nonassociativity tensor ωijkl of a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) of dimension
n ≥ 3 be the completely trace-free part of µijkl ,
                                                                  
                                          2                               2
(6.14)                   ωijkl = µijkl + n−2  ρk[i hj]l − ρl[i hj]k + (n−1)(n−2) σhl[i hj]k ,

in which σ = hij ρij is the metric trace of the Ricci form. Equivalently,
              ω(x, y, z, w) = h(y ◦ z, x ◦ w) − h(z ◦ x, w ◦ y)
                               1
(6.15)                      + n−2 (ρ(x, z)h(y, w) − ρ(y, z)h(x, w) − ρ(x, w)h(y, z) + ρ(y, w)h(x, z))
                                  σ
                            + (n−1)(n−2) (h(x, w)h(y, z) − h(y, w)h(x, z)) .

If ωijkl vanishes then (A, ◦) is conformally associative. Since the O(n)-module of trace-free tensors with
metric curvature symmetries is trivial if n ≤ 3, in three dimensions this is automatic. The convention is
adopted that a two-dimensional metrized commutative algebra is regarded as conformally associative.
Lemma 6.6. If char k = 0, a conformally associative metrized commutative k-algebra (A, ◦, h) of dimension
n ≥ 3 is projectively associative if and only if there is c ∈ k such that ρ◦ = (n − 1)ch.

       positive-definite Killing form τ◦ , and is projectively associative.

unitalization of (A, ◦). By Lemma 6.2, (Â, ˆ◦) is associative if and only if (A, ◦) is projectively associative, in
which case, by the exactness of (A, ◦),

Example 6.9. For c ∈ k× , define a commutative multiplication ◦ on the k-vector space V generated by
{e1 , e2 , e3 } by

and extending linearly. It is straightforward to check that (V, ◦) is a projectively associative Killing metrized

Corollary 6.10. A Killing metrized exact commutative k-algebra (A, ◦) of dimension n ∈ {2, 3} is isomorphic
to En (k) if either k is algebraically closed or k = R and τ◦ is positive definite.
Proof. Since n ≤ 3, (A, ◦, τ◦ ) is conformally associative, so projectively associative by Lemma 6.6, and the
conclusion follows from Theorem 6.8.                                                                       
Example 6.11. By Example 2.7, the conclusion of Corollary 6.10 is false over Q. Were the deunitalization
of the degree 3 cyclic Galois extension of Q obtained by adjoining a root of r3 + r2 − 2r − 1 isomorphic to
E2 (Q), then its unitalization would be isomorphic to a direct sum of three copies of Q, which it is not, so, by
Lemma 5.3, it cannot be isomorphic to E2 (Q).                                                                  ⊳
Lemma 6.12. A Killing metrized exact commutative algebra (A, ◦) has no τ◦ -anisotropic ideal of dimension
1 or codimension 1.
Proof. A τ◦ -anisotropic subspace I ⊂ A is an ideal if and only if its τ◦ -orthogonal complement I⊥ is an
ideal. In this case A = I ⊕ I⊥ and I ◦ I⊥ = {0}. Suppose I ⊂ A is a one-dimensional τ◦ -anisotropic ideal
spanned by z ∈ A. Because L◦ (z)I⊥ = {0} and tr L◦ (z) = 0, z ◦ z = 0 and so L◦ (z) = 0. Consequently,
τ◦ (z, x) = tr L◦ (z)L◦ (x) = 0 for all x ∈ A, contradicting the nondegeneracy of τ◦ .                  
Corollary 6.13. A 3-dimensional Killing metrized exact commutative algebra with anisotropic Killing form
is simple.
Lemma 6.14. If a 4-dimensional Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative algebra (A, ◦) is not simple,
then it is isomorphic to E2 (R) ⊕ E2 (R).
Proof. If (A, ◦) has a two-dimensional ideal, then by Corollary 6.10 this ideal and its orthogonal complement
must be isomorphic to E2 (R), so it suffices to show that (A, ◦) has no odd-dimensional ideal. Since the
orthcomplement of an ideal is an ideal, it suffices to show that (A, ◦) has no one-dimensional ideal. Suppose
that z ∈ A generates a one-dimensional ideal. Then z ◦ z = rz for some r ∈ R. Because L◦ (z) maps the
orthocomplement of z into the space of z and is exact, r = tr L◦ (z) = 0. Then L◦ (z)2 = 0, so τ◦ (z, z) = 0,
contradicting the anisotropy of τ◦ .                                                                       
Theorem 6.15. A Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative algebra of dimension n ≤ 4 is isomorphic
to exactly one of E2 (R), E3 (R), E4 (R), or E2 (R) ⊕ E2 (R).
Proof. By Corollary 6.10 and Lemma 6.14 it suffices to show that a simple 4-dimensional Euclidean Killing
metrized exact commutative algebra is isomorphic to E4 (R). This follows from [27, Theorem 1.8], which gives
normal forms for the cubic polynomial of a 4-dimensional Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative
algebra.                                                                                                  
Remark 6.16. It is desirable to find a purely algebraic proof that a simple 4-dimensional Euclidean Killing
metrized exact commutative algebra is isomorphic to E4 (R).                                                    ⊳
    A consequence of Theorem 6.15 is that for a Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative algebra to have
a positive-dimensional automorphism group it is necessary that its dimension be at least 5. The examples
of Section 9 show that this does occur.
    It is desirable to have a direct proof that positive-dimensional automorphism group implies the dimension
of the algebra is at least 5. Corollary 6.19 is a result in this direction. It shows that the automorphism group
of a Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative algebra is finite when n ≤ 4, provided that the algebra
is spanned by its minimal idempotents.
    A derivation of (A, ◦) is an endomorphism D ∈ End(A) satisfying [D, L◦ (x)] = L◦ (Dx) for all x ∈ A. The
derivations of (A, ◦) constitute a Lie subalgebra Der(A, ◦) ⊂ End(A) with the usual commutator bracket. A
derivation of (A, ◦) need not be h-anti-self-adjoint, although a derivation of a Killing metrized commutative
algebra is necessarily τ◦ -anti-self-adjoint.
                   COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                    25

                                                                        / Π(e), then De = 0, so
[D, L◦ (e)] = L◦ (De) = 0, and D preserves the eigenspaces of L◦ (e).                        

Lemma 6.18. Let Der(A, ◦) be the Lie algebra of algebra derivations of an n-dimensional Euclidean metrized
exact commutative algebra (A, ◦, h). If there is e ∈ M(A, ◦, h) such that k = dim{D(e) : D ∈ Der(A, ◦)} > 0,
then n ≥ 2k + 3.
Proof. By hypothesis there are e ∈ M(A, ◦, h) and D1 , . . . , Dk ∈ Der(A, ◦) such that D1 (e), . . . , Dk (e) are
linearly independent. By Lemma 6.17, D1 (e), . . . , Dk (e) ∈ A(1/2) (e), so 1/2 has multiplicity at least k as
an eigenvalue of L◦ (e). Let λ1 , . . . , λn−1−k be the remaining (not necessarily distinct) elements of Π(e).
                        Pn−1−k
Because (A, ◦) is exact, i=1     λi = −(k + 2)/2. Since e is minimal, by (2b) of Lemma 2.13, λi ≤ 1/2 for
1 ≤ i ≤ n − 1 − k. Hence, by the Schwarz inequality,

Corollary 6.19.
    (1) If a Euclidean metrized exact commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) admits D ∈ Der(A, ◦), then either
        D(M(A, ◦, h)) = {0} or dim A ≥ 5.
    (2) If a Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative algebra (A, ◦) is spanned by its minimal idempo-
        tents and admits a nontrivial D ∈ Der(A, ◦), then dim A ≥ 5.
Proof. The first claim is immediate from Lemma 6.18. Suppose (A, ◦) is a Euclidean Killing metrized
exact commutative algebra spanned by M(A, ◦, τ◦ ) and D ∈ Der(A, ◦). If D(M(A, ◦, h)) = {0}, then, for
                                                                                             ⊥
e ∈ M(A, ◦, τ◦ ), 0 = τ◦ (De, x) = −τ◦ (e, Dx) for all x ∈ A, so D(A) ⊂ (Span M(A, ◦, τ◦ )) = A⊥ = {0},
showing D is trivial. Consequently, if D is nontrivial, then, by the first claim, dim A ≥ 5.         

                    7. Quantifying nonassociativity: sectional nonassociativity
   By the Beltrami theorem (see [18, section 4]), the Levi-Civita connection of a Riemannian metric is
projectively flat if and only if the metric has constant sectional curvature. Here there is introduced a notion
of sectional nonassociativity, modeled on the sectional curvature, such that the formally analogous statement
for Killing metrized exact commutative algebras holds. This notion of sectional nonassociativity facilitates
quantifications of nonassociativity that are explored in Section 8.
Definition 7.1. Let (A, ◦, h) be a metrized commutative algebra of dimension at least 2. Define the sectional
nonassociativity K(x, y) of the h-nondegenerate subspace Span{x, y} ⊂ A to be
                                           −µ(x, y, x, y)          −h([y, x, x], y)           h([x, x, y], y)
             K(x, y) = K◦,h (x, y) =                           =                        =
                                        |x|2h |y|2h − h(x, y)2   |x|2h |y|2h − h(x, y)2   |x|2h |y|2h − h(x, y)2
(7.1)
                       h(x ◦ x, y ◦ y) − h(x ◦ y, y ◦ x)
                     =                                       ,
                             |x|2h |y|2h − h(x, y)2
where |x|2h = h(x, x), |x ∧ y|2h = 2(|x|2h |y|2h − h(x, y)2 ), and µ is as in (6.13).
Lemma 7.2. Let (A, ◦, h) be a metrized commutative algebra of dimension at least 2. The quantity K(x, y)
defined by (7.1) for linearly independent x, y ∈ A spanning an h-nondegenerate subspace depends only on the
subspace spanned by x and y.
26                                                   DANIEL J. F. FOX


Proof. If x̄ = ax + by and ȳ = cx + dy span the subspace spanned by x and y then
                 h(x̄ ◦ x̄, ȳ ◦ ȳ) − h(x̄ ◦ ȳ, x̄ ◦ ȳ) = (ad − bc)2 (h(x ◦ x, y ◦ y) − h(x ◦ y, y ◦ x)) ,
(7.2)                                                                                       
                 h(x̄, ȳ)2 − h(x̄, x̄)h(ȳ, ȳ) = (ad − bc)2 h(x, y)2 − h(x, x)h(y, y) ,
from which it is apparent that K(x̄, ȳ) = K(x, y), so depends only on the span of x and y.                        
                                                                                                                   ×
    The dependence of K = K◦,h on ◦ and h is indicated with subscripts when helpful. For r, s ∈ k ,
Ks◦,rh = s2 r−1 K◦,h , where s◦ means the multiplcation sx ◦ y. When ◦ is scaled by s, τ◦ rescales by s2 , and
it follows that K◦,τ◦ is insensitive to such rescalings. This motivates Definition 7.3.
Definition 7.3. The intrinsic sectional nonassociativity of a two-dimensional τ◦ -nondegenerate subspace of
a Killing metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦) spanned by x, y ∈ A is
                                                          τ◦ (x ◦ x, y ◦ y) − τ◦ (x ◦ y, y ◦ x)
(7.3)                       T◦ (x, y) = K◦,τ◦ (x, y) =                                          .
                                                              τ◦ (x, x)τ◦ (y, y) − τ◦ (x, y)2
  The intrinsic sectional nonassociativity is an isomorpism invariant of Killing metrized commutative alge-
bras.
  A Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) has constant sectional nonassociativity κ if there is a
constant κ ∈ R such that K(x, y) = κ for all subspaces Span{x, y} ⊂ A.
Example 7.4. The simplicial algebra (En (R), ◦, τ◦ ) has constant sectional nonassociativity −1/(n − 1). ⊳
Lemma 7.5. A Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) of dimension at least 2 has constant sec-
tional nonassociativity κ if and only if
(7.4)                                      [x, y, z] = κ (h(x, y)z − h(y, z)x)
for all x, y, z ∈ A. In this case, ρ◦ = κ(dim A − 1)h.
Proof. If there holds (7.4), then K(x, y) = κ by (7.1). Suppose a tensor aijkl has the symmetries aijkl =
−ajikl = −aijlk and a[ijk]l = 0. If there holds xi y j xk y l aijkl = 0 for all x, y ∈ A, then
(7.5)                            0 = aijkl + akjil + ailkj + aklij = 2aijkl − 2ajkil ,
so that aijkl = ajkil = akijl . Consequently 3aijkl = 3aijkl − 3a[ijk]l = 2aijkl − ajkil − akijl = 0. That (A, ◦, h)
has constant sectional nonassociativity κ means that xi y j xk y l (2κhk[i hj]l − µijkl ) = 0 for all x, y ∈ A. Taking
aijkl = µijkl in (7.5) yields µijkl = 2κhk[i hj]l , which implies (7.4). If there holds (7.4), then
(7.6)                         L◦ (x ◦ y) − L◦ (x)L◦ (y) = κ(h(x, y) IdA −x ⊗ h(y, · ))
and tracing (7.6) shows ρ◦ = κ(dim A − 1)h.                                                                        
   More generallly, a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) over k, with h possibly isotropic is defined to have
constant sectional nonassociativity if there holds (7.4). This implies that that the sectional nonassociativity
of any h-nondegenerate subspace is equal to κ.
Corollary 7.6. A metrized commutative R-algebra (A, ◦, h) of dimension at least 2 has constant sectional
nonassociativity κ if and only if it is projectively associative with ρ◦ = κ(dim A − 1)h.
Proof. This is immediate from Lemmas 6.2 and 7.5.                                                                  
   Lemma 7.7 is the analogue of the theorem of Beltrami. It yields that a metrized commutative algebra has
zero sectional nonassociativity if and only if it is associative.
Lemma 7.7. For an n-dimensional Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) the following are equiv-
alent:
    (1) It has constant sectional nonassociativity c.
    (2) It is conformally associative and there is c ∈ R such that ρ◦ = (n − 1)ch.
    (3) It is projectively and conformally associative.
In particular:
                   COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                      27


    (4) Such an algebra has vanishing sectional nonassociativity if and only if it is associative.
    (5) Such an algebra is Killing metrized with Killing Einstein constant κ = −(n − 1)c if and only if it is
        exact. In this case, if κ 6= 0, it is isomorphic to En (R).
Proof. Lemma 6.6 shows the equivalence of (2) and (3) and its proof shows that these conditions imply
(1). By (7.4) of Lemma 7.5, if (A, ◦, h) has constant sectional nonassociativity, then µijkl = 2chl[i hj]k . This
implies [x, y, z] = −ch(y, z)x + ch(x, y)z, so there holds [x, y, z] = −ch(y, z)x + ch(x, y)z and ◦ is projectively
associative. Also, in this case, ρij = ρ̄ji = 2µpij p = (n − 1)chij and σ = n(n − 1)c. In (6.14) there results
ωijkl = 0. This shows (1) implies (2) and (3). Claim (4) follows from (7.4) of Lemma 7.5.
   If an algebra satisfies (1)-(3) with κ 6= 0, then it is Ricci metrized by Lemma 6.2. If it is moreover exact, this
implies it is Killing metrized. If it is also Killing metrized, then the invariance of both the Ricci and Killing
forms implies 0 = tr L◦ ([x, y, z]) for all x, y, z ∈ A. By (7.4) this implies h(x, y) tr L◦ (x) = h(y, z) tr L◦ (x) for
all x, y, z ∈ A. Choosing y = x and z such that h(x, z) = 0, there results |x|2h tr L◦ (z) = 0. This shows (A, ◦)
is exact and proves the first part of claim (5). That in this case, if κ 6= 0, the algebra is isomorphic to En (R)
follows from Theorem 6.8.                                                                                             

   Lemma 7.8 shows that unitalization increases sectional nonassociativity.
Lemma 7.8. For the deunitalization (A, ◦, h) of a unital metrized commutative algebra (B, ⋆, g) with non-
istropic unit e, for all x, y ∈ A with h-nondegenerate span there holds
(7.7)                                   g(e, e)K(B,⋆,g) (x, y) = K(A,◦,h) (x, y) + 1.

Proof. This follows from a computation using the definition of the deunitalization and (7.1).                        

     8. Bounds on sectional nonassociativity: generalizations of the Norton inequality
   In Section 6 it was shown that certain quasi-associativity conditions modeled on notions of projective
and conformal flatness for connections on vector bundles facilitate characterization of the simplicial algebras.
This suggests further developing the analogy between the structure tensor of a metrized commutative algebra
and a Levi-Civita connection with the idea of mimicking notions more refined than flatness, such as one-sided
curvature bounds and curvature pinching.
   Over k = R, a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) has positive, nonnegative, zero, etc.
sectional nonassociativity if the given qualifier is valid for every two-dimensional subspace of A. Lemma
8.1 shows that taking direct sums of Euclidean metrized commutative algebras preserves conditions such as
nonpositive and nonnegative sectional nonassociativity.
Lemma 8.1. Let (A, ◦, h) be a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra and suppose that A = A1 ⊕ A2 is a
decomposition into h-orthogonal ideals.
    (1) If (A1 , ◦, h) and (A2 , ◦, h) have sectional nonassociativities bounded below by constants m1 and m2 ,
        then (A, ◦, h) has sectional nonassociativity bounded below by min{m1 , m2 , 0}.
    (2) If (A1 , ◦, h) and (A2 , ◦, h) have sectional nonassociativities bounded above by constants M1 and M2 ,
        then (A, ◦, h) has sectional nonassociativity bounded above by max{M1 , M2 , 0}.
In particular, if (A1 , ◦, h) and (A2 , ◦, h) have nonpositive (resp. nonnegative) sectional nonassociativity, then
(A, ◦, h) has nonpositive (resp. nonnegative) sectional nonassociativity.
Proof. For x, y ∈ A, write |x|2 = |x|2h and hx, yi = h(x, y). Observe that, for any x1 , x2 , y1 , y2 ∈ A,
                       |x1 |2 |y2 |2 + |x2 |2 |y1 |2 − 2hx1 , y1 ihx2 , y2 i
                              = 12 ||x1 |y2 − |y1 |x2 |2 + 21 ||x2 |y1 − |y2 |x1 |2
(8.1)
                                + (|x1 ||y1 | − hx1 , y1 i) hx2 , y2 i + (|x2 ||y2 | − hx2 , y2 i) hx1 , y1 i
                              ≥ 21 ||x1 |y2 − |y1 |x2 |2 + 21 ||x2 |y1 − |y2 |x1 |2 ≥ 0.

Let xi ∈ Ai be the h-orthogonal projection of x ∈ A. Note that hx1 ∧ y1 , x2 ∧ y2 i = |x1 |2 |y2 |2 + |x2 |2 |y1 |2 −

By Lemma 8.3 thee estimates yield sharp numerical bounds on the sectional nonassociativites of subspaces
spanned by decomposable elements in algebras such as so(m) ⊗ so(n), so(m) ⊗ su(n), and su(m) ⊗ su(n).
   Note that Lemma 8.3 does not imply that g⊗h has nonpositive sectional nonassociativity. In fact, Theorem
11.18 shows that, for any compact simple real Lie algebra g, so(3) ⊗ g has sectional nonassociativities of both
signs.                                                                                                        ⊳
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                 29


   From (7.1) it is apparent that a metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) has nonnegative sectional nonas-
sociativity if and only if for all x, y ∈ A there holds
(8.9)                           0 ≤ h([x, x, y], y) = h(x ◦ x, y ◦ y) − h(x ◦ y, x ◦ y).
The inequality (8.9) is known as Norton’s inequality. Similarly, nonpositive sectional nonassociativity is
equivalent to the reverse Norton’s inequality, which is (8.9) with the inequality reversed. From the point of
view taken here, Norton’s inequality is analogous to nonpositive sectional curvature. The notion of sectional
nonassociativity facilitates quantifications of nonassociativity more refined than Norton’s inequality.
   For a different perspective on the importance of the Norton inequality, see the discussion of Majorana
involutions and axial vectors in [44, Chapter 8.6].
Example 8.4. A fundamental fact about the 196884-dimensional Conway-Griess algebra described in Exam-
ple 5.6, established in [19, section 16], is that it satisfies Norton’s inequality (8.9). Hence it has nonnegative
sectional nonassociativity, and by (7.7) it follows that the 196883-dimensional nonunital Griess algebra
(A, ⋆, h) has sectional nonassociativity greater than or equal to −1.                                            ⊳
Example 8.5. By [65, Theorem 6.3], if an OZ VOA defined over R has a positive definite bilinear form, then
the Norton inequality holds on its Griess algebra. In the language used here this means that such a Griess
algebra has nonnegative sectional nonassociativity. In particular this means that the (unital) Griess algebra
of the moonshine VOA has nonnegative sectional nonassociativity.                                                 ⊳
