Corollary 8.7. In a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) with nonpositive sectional nonasso-
ciativity, any element of I(A ⊗R C, ◦) or N(A ⊗R C, ◦) is a multiple of an element of I(A, ◦) or N(A, ◦).
Lemma 8.8. If in a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) there is a C 1 immersion x : (−a, a) →
A such that x(t) ∈ I(A, ◦) for all t ∈ (−a, a) and ẋ(0) 6= 0, then 1/2 ∈ Π(x(0)) and
                                                                 1
(8.14)                                       K(x(0), ẋ(0)) = 4|x(0)| 2 > 0.
                                                                            h


Proof. Differentiating x ◦ x = x shows that ẋ(0) is an eigenvector of L◦ (x(0)) with eigenvalue 1/2. As
h(x, ẋ) = 2h(x, x ◦ ẋ) = 2h(x ◦ x, ẋ) = 2h(x, ẋ), there holds h(x, ẋ) = 0, so x(0) and ẋ(0) are linearly
independent. The identity (8.14) follows from (7.1).                                                        
Lemma 8.9. Let (A, ◦, h) be a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h).
  (1) If (A, ◦, h) has negative sectional nonassociativity, then Aut(A, ◦, h) is finite.
30                                                DANIEL J. F. FOX


     (2) If (A, ◦, h) has nonpositive sectional nonassociativity, either Aut(A, ◦, h) is finite or (A, ◦, h) contains
         a trivial two-dimensional subalgebra (the possibilities are not exclusive).
Proof. For k ≥ 3, let θ be a multilinear k-form on a vector space V over an algebraically closed field of
characteristic zero. By a theorem of H. Suzuki [76, Theorem B], either the group of linear automorphisms
of θ is finite or there is a nonzero vector v ∈ V such that θ(v, . . . , v, w) = 0 for all w ∈ V. Regard
µ(x, y, z) = h(x ◦ y, z) as a cubic form on the complexification V = A ⊗R C. An orthogonal automorphism of
(A, ◦, h) preserves µ, and extends to an automorphism of the complexified algebra. By Suzuki’s theorem if
Aut(A, ◦, h) is not finite there exists a + ib ∈ A ⊗R C such that h((a + ib) ◦ (a + ib), w) = 0 for all w ∈ A ⊗R C.
By the nondegeneracy of h, a + ib ∈ N(A ⊗R C, ◦). The conclusion follows from (1) of Lemma 8.6.                  
Example 8.10. Let g and h be Lie algebras over a field k of characteristic zero. Let c ⊂ g be an abelian
subalgebra and let k ⊂ h be any subalgebra. Then c ⊗k k ⊂ g ⊗k h is a trivial subalgebra. In particular, if g
and h are semisimple Lie algebras over a field of characteristic zero and c is a Cartan subalgebra, then c ⊗k h
is a large trivial subalgebra of the Killing metrized exact commutative algebra g ⊗k h.                       ⊳
   This section concludes with some results showing that nonnegative sectional nonassociativity prevents the
existence of square-zero elements. Example 8.11 shows that some condition is necessary for such a conclusion.
Example 8.11. For a Killing metrized exact commutative algebra, N(A, ◦) can be large. Let g and h be Lie
algebras over a field k of characteristic zero. In g ⊗k h, every decomposable element is square-zero. More
generally, for any a ∈ g, khai ⊗k h is a trivial subalgebra.                                             ⊳
Lemma 8.12. Let (A, ◦, h) be a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra. Let 0 6= z ∈ N(A, ◦). For 0 6= y ∈ A
not in the span of z, K(z, y) ≤ 0, with equality if and only if z ◦ y = 0. In particular, N(A, ◦) = {0} if there
holds any of the following conditions:
    (1) (A, ◦, h) has positive sectional nonassociativity.
    (2) (A, ◦, h) has nonnegative sectional nonassociativity and is semisimple.
    (3) (A, ◦, h) has nonnegative sectional nonassociativity and positive definite Killing form.
Proof. Let 0 6= z ∈ N(A, ◦). For all y ∈ A not in Span{z}, there holds K(z, y)(|z|2h |y|2h − h(z, y)2 ) =
−|L◦ (z)y|2h ≤ 0, with equality if and only if z ◦ y = 0. In particular, (A, ◦, h) cannot have positive sectional
nonassociativity, and if (A, ◦, h) has nonnegative sectional nonassociativity, then L◦ (z)y = 0 for all y ∈ A, so
L◦ (z) = 0. By Lemma 2.3, if L◦ (z) = 0, then (A, ◦) is not semisimple. Similarly, τ◦ is not positive definite,
for L◦ (z) = 0 implies τ◦ (z, z) = tr L◦ (z)2 = 0.                                                             
Corollary 8.13. A Euclidean Killing metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦) with nonnegative sectional nonas-
sociativity contains no nontrivial square-zero elements.
Proof. By Lemma 2.5, (A, ◦) is semisimple, so the claim follows from (2) or (3) of Lemma 8.12.                     

                      9. Deunitalizations of simple Euclidean Jordan algebras
  An k-algebra (A, ◦) is Jordan if it is commutative and satisfies [x ◦ x, y, x] = 0 for all x, y ∈ A. For the
background on Jordan algebras used here and in what follows see [3, 24, 46, 72, 75].
  A Jordan algebra is semisimple if and only if the invariant symmetric bilinear form tr L◦ (x ◦ y) is nonde-
generate [72, section IV.1], in which case the Jordan algebra is necessarily unital.
Lemma 9.1. Let char k = 0 and let (A, ◦, h) be a metrized commutative k-algebra with unitalization (Â, ˆ◦, ĥ).
                          ◦) is Jordan if and only if for all x, y ∈ A there holds

     (3) If (A, ◦, h) satisfies (9.1), its unitalization is a semisimple Jordan algebra.

   An algebra (A, ◦) over a field of characteristic zero is power associative if and only if [x, x, x] = 0 (which is
automatic for a commutative algebra) and [x ◦ x, x, x] = 0 for all x ∈ A. Since char k = 0 is assumed, taking
z = y = x in (6.7) and y = x in (9.3) shows that (Â, ◦) is power associative if and only if there holds (9.2).

Remark 9.2. Claim (1) of Lemma 9.1 is valid over any field, but claims (2) and (3) are not. Since a
commutative algebra over a field of characteristic not dividing 30 is power associative if and only if [x ◦
x, x, x] = 0 [72, Chapter 5], claim (2) is true in this case, but can fail if char k divides 30. Claim (3) fails
because the characterization of semisimplicity in terms of the nondegeneracy of the form tr L( · ˆ◦ · ) fails. ⊳
Remark 9.3. If char k = 0, claims 1 and 2 of Lemma 9.18 are redundant. A Jordan algebra is power
associative. Conversely, by the main theorem of [50], combined with results from [2], a simple commutative
power associative algebra over a field of characteristic zero is Jordan.                                          ⊳
    A Jordan algebra is Euclidean if it is real and admits an invariant positive definite symmetric bilinear
form. The rank of an element x in a real or complex Jordan algebra with unit e is the smallest integer k such
that {e, x, x2 , . . . , xk } is linearly dependent (this uses that a Jordan algebra is power associative), and the
rank r of the Jordan algebra is the maximum of the ranks of its elements. In a unital Jordan algebra, a set of
primitive orthogonal idempotents is complete if it sums to the unit. For a simple Euclidean Jordan algebra,
the rank r is the number of elements in a complete system of primitive orthogonal idempotents {e1 , . . . , er },
and the Peirce invariant d is the common dimension of the image of L◦ (ei )L◦ (ej ) for i 6= j (equivalently, it
is the dimension of the intersection of the 1/2-eigenspaces of any two orthogonal primitive idempotents; see
[24, chapter V ]). It is related to the rank and the dimension N by the equivalent formulas (see [24, p. 71])

(9.4)                        N = r + dr(r−1)
                                        2    ,                               d = 2(N −r)
                                                                                 r(r−1) .

Every simple Euclidean Jordan algebra over the real numbers having rank at least two is isomorphic to one
of the Jordan algebras listed in table 3. The algebra (Rn ⊕ R, ⋆) is the unitalization of the trivial algebra
(Rn , ◦) with respect to the standard Euclidean metric. It is often called a spin factor.
   A unital composition algebra is also called a Hurwitz algebra. Let M(n, k) denote the n × n matrices over
the real Hurwitz algebra k ∈ {R, C, H, O}. The subspace of n × n Hermitian matrices over k is the subspace
Herm(n, k) = {x ∈ M(n, k) : x̄t = x} comprising the fixed points of the conjugate transpose x → x̄t , where
x → x̄ is the involution of k determined by its structure as a composition algebra and fixing the real subfield
(so is trivial when k = R). When k = O there is always supposed n = 3. The space Herm(n, k) is a Jordan
algebra when equipped with the symmetrized matrix product x ⋆ y = 12 (xy + yx), where juxtaposition, as in
the expression xy, indicates the matrix product in Herm(n, R).
   The following facts are used in what follows.
    (1) Because a Jordan algebra is power associative, the trace tr x and determinant det x of an element
        x ∈ B can be defined as the coefficients of the subleading term and the zeroth order term in the
        minimal polynomial of x. The trace and determinant of a real Jordan algebra (B, ⋆) are invariant
        under Aut(B, ⋆). See [24, Proposition II.4.2].
    (2) For an element x of a rank r Euclidean Jordan algebra (B, ⋆), there is a complete system of orthogonal
        idempotents, {e1 , . . . , er }, such that x ∈ Span{e1 , . . . , er }. See [24, Theorem III.1.1].
    (3) Given two complete systems of orthogonal idempotents, {e1 , . . . , er } and {f1 , . . . , fr }, in a simple
        Euclidean Jordan algebra (B, ⋆) of rank r, there is g ∈ Aut(B, ⋆) such that gei = fi for 1 ≤ i ≤ r.
        See [24, Theorem IV.2.5].
32                                                 DANIEL J. F. FOX


     (4) (Principal axis theorem) Every element of Herm(n, k) (where n = 3 if k = O) is equivalent via an
         element of Aut(Herm(n, k), ⋆) to a diagonal matrix. For k ∈ {R, C, H} this is well known. For k = O
         this is [32, Theorem 5.1]; see also [24, Theorem V.2.5].

                                 Table 3. Simple Euclidean Jordan algebras

                 Description                               Notation       Dimension      Rank       d

                 Euclidean spin factor                      Rn ⊕ R           n+1            2     n−1
                 Real symmetric matrices                  Herm(n, R)      n(n + 1)/2       n        1
                 Complex Hermitian matrices               Herm(n, C)           n2          n        2
                                                                              2
                 Quaternionic Hermitian matrices Herm(n, H)                2n − n          n        4
                 Octonionic Hermitian matrices            Herm(3, O)           27           3       8

   If a Jordan algebra (B, ⋆) is simple, then any invariant metric is a multiple of the invariant metric g(x, y) =
tr L⋆ (x ⋆ y). Thus the deunitalization (A, ◦, h) of a simple unital Euclidean Jordan algebra (B, ⋆) means
unambiguously its deunitalization with respect to g. For a simple Euclidean Jordan algebra (B, ⋆) of rank
r ≥ 2 and dimension N , the unit satisfies tr L⋆ (e) = N and the deunitalization of (B, ⋆) is defined using
ĥ(x, y) = N −1 tr L⋆ (x ⋆ y) = r−1 tr(x ⋆ y). The deunitalization of Herm(n, k) is the subspace Herm0 (n, k) =
{x ∈ Herm(n, k) : tr x = 0} equipped with the product ◦, invariant metric h, and cubic polynomial P given
by
                                                                                           
                         x ◦ y = x ⋆ y − n−1 tr(x ⋆ y)I = 21 xy + yx − n−1 tr(xy + yx)I ,
                                               1
(9.5)                h(x, y) = n1 tr(x ⋆ y) = 2n tr(xy + yx) = n1 Re tr(xy),
                                1
                      6P (x) = 2n tr((x2 )x + x(x2 )) = n1 Re tr((x2 )x),
where juxtaposition, as in xy or x(x2 ), indicates the matrix product in Herm(n, R), and I is the identity
matrix in Herm(n, R). It is necessary to write x(x2 ) + (x2 )x and not 2x3 because the matrix multiplication
in Herm(3, O) is not associative, although it is true that x(x2 ) − (x2 )x is a multiple of the identity by an
element of O [24, Lemma V.2.4]. Note also that Re tr(xy) = Re tr(yx) and Re tr((xy)z) = Re tr(x(yz)) are
true for n × n matrices over k [24, Proposition V.2.1].
Remark 9.4. In [67, section 3], the simple Euclidean Jordan algebras are constructed as the unitalizations
of the nonunital algebras Herm0 (n, k) equipped with the multiplication ◦ and metric h defined in (9.5) (see
Remark 3.3 and equations 3.27a and 3.27b in Remark 3.4 of [67]).                                             ⊳

Lemma 9.7. Suppose k is a real Hurwitz algebra and n ≥ 3, with equality if k = O. The diagonal subalgebra
(B, ◦, h) ⊂ (Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h) is isometrically isomorphic to (En−1 (R), (n − 2)τ ).
Proof. Let eii be the n × n matrix with a 1 in the ii position and all other entries 0. The elements
                                                 n
                                                                
(9.8)                                   γ(i) = n−2    eii − n1 I ∈ B
satisfy γ(i) ◦ γ(i) = γ(i) and (2 − n)γ(i) ◦ γ(j) = γ(i) + γ(j) for i 6= j. By Corollary 3.7, this suffices
                                                                                                          n
to show that (B, ◦) is isomorphic to En−1 (R). Straightforward computations show h(γ(i), γ(i)) = (n−2)       2
                         1
and h(γ(i), γ(j)) = − (n−2)2 , so that, by (3.4), (B, ◦, h) is isomorphic as a metrized commutative algebra to
(En−1 (R), (n − 2)τ ).                                                                                      

Lemma 9.8. The deunitalization of a simple Euclidean Jordan algebra of rank r ≥ 3 is simple.
Proof. Such an algebra has the form (Herm0 (n, k), ◦) where k is a real Hurwitz algebra, n ≥ 3, and n = 3
if k = O. Let J ⊂ (Herm0 (n, k), ◦) be a nontrivial ideal. By the principal axis theorem, a nonzero x ∈ J is
equivalent to a diagonal element of Herm0 (n, k) via an element of Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦). Since the image of J
under an automorphism of (Herm0 (n, k), ◦) is again an ideal, without loss of generality it can be supposed
that J contains some element of the diagonal subalgebra (B, ◦) ⊂ (Herm0 (n, k), ◦). Then J ∩ B is a nontrivial
ideal in (B, ⊙). By Lemma 9.7, (B, ◦) is isomorphic to En−1 (R), which is simple by Corollary 3.7, so J ∩B = B.
That is, J contains B. Together with B, Herm0 (n, k) is spanned as a vector space by elements of the form
zeij + z̄eji where eij is the elementary matrix with a 1 in the ij entry and 0 in every other entry, z ∈ k, and
i 6= j. The matrix Dij = eii + ejj − 2ekk is in B, and so zeij + z̄eji = (zeij + z̄eji ) ◦ Dij ∈ B. This proves
Herm0 (n, k) = J.                                                                                             

· · · + γ(n − 1) + γ(n) = 0. As a consequence of the proof of Lemma 9.7, the products γ(I) ◦ γ(J) are given by

Lemma 9.9. Suppose k is a real Hurwitz algebra and n ≥ 3, with n = 3 if k = O.
  (1) If X ∈ N(Herm0 (n, k), ◦), then X is in the Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) orbit of a matrix of the form cγ(I)
      with 0 6= c ∈ k and |I| = n/2. In particular, if n is odd then N(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) = {0}.
  (2) I(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) is the union of the Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) orbits of the matrices E(I) where I ⊂
      {1, . . . , n} satisfies 1 ≤ |I| ≤ n − 1 and 2|I| 6= n. Moreover, E(I) and E(J) are in the same
      Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) orbit if and only if |I| = |J| or |I| = n − |J|.
  (3) The idempotent E(I) satisfies |E(I)|2h = |I|(n−|I|)                 2         2
                                                 (n−2|I|)2 , and so |E(I)|h < |E(J)|h if ||I|−n/2| > ||J|−n/2|.
      Consequently the idempotents in (Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h) having minimal norm are the elements in the
      Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) orbits of E(i), 1 ≤ i ≤ n.
  (4) A minimal idempotent in (Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h) has a positive eigenvalue on its orthogonal complement.
Proof. Every element of Herm0 (n, k) is Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) equivalent to a diagonal matrix. By Lemma 9.7,
the subalgebra (B, ◦, h) ⊂ (Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h) of diagonal matrices is isomorphic as a metrized commutative
algebra to (En−1 (R), (n − 2)τ ). The claims (1)-(3) follow from the description of the idempotents and square-
zero elements of En−1 (R) given in Lemma 3.16. By (2) and (3), a minimal norm idempotent is in the
Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) orbit of E(n). Since Aut(Herm0 (n, k), ◦) acts transitively on this orbit, to show (4) it
                                                                                      Pn−1
suffices to show that Π(E(n)) contains a positive number. For xi ∈ R define x = i=1         xi (ein + eni ). Then
h(x, E(n)) = 0 and E(n) ◦ x = (1/2)x, which proves the claim.                                                  
Remark 9.10. As an element of the diagonal subalgebra of (Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h), the orthogonal spectrum
of E(i) is contained in (−∞, 0]), but (4) shows that with respect to the full algebra (Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h) the
orthogonal spectrum contains 1/2.                                                                            ⊳
   There is an alternative description of (Herm0 (n, C), ◦). Let SU (n) be the special unitary group and let
su(n) be its Lie algebra, regarded as the space of n × n skew-Hermitian matrices su(n) = {x ∈ M(n, C) : x =
−x̄t }. The Lie Killing form is Bsu(n) (x, y) = 2n tr xy. Take h(x, y) = −2n−2 Bsu(n) (x, y) = −n−1 tr(xy) as
the metric. Define a commutative, nonassociative multiplication ⊙ on su(n) by
                                                                       
(9.10)                                x ⊙ y = 2i xy + yx − n2 tr(xy)I ,
where I is the n × n identity matrix. The map Ψ : su(n) → Herm0 (n, C) defined by Ψ(x) = ix is an isometric
isomorphism from (su(n), ⊙, h) to (Herm0 (n, C), ◦, h).
   The multiplication (9.10) appears in [53], where it is used in the construction of a nontrivial family of
bi-invariant affine connections on SU (n). See also [8].
   The adjoint action Ad of SU (n) on su(n), Ad(g)X = gXg −1 preserves h and ⊙, so Ad induces an injective
homomorphism SU (n) → Aut(su(n), ⊙, h). However Ad(SU (n)) is contained properly in Aut(su(n), ⊙, h),
for the outer Lie algebra antiautomorphism Θ(x) = −x̄ of (su(n), [ · , · ]) is an h-isometric automorphism of
(su(n), ⊙). Lemma 9.11 shows that Ad(SU (n)) and Θ generate Aut(su(n), ◦).
Lemma 9.11. The adjoint action of SU (n) on (su(n), [ · , · ]) and the outer automorphism Θ(X) = −X̄
generate the group Aut(su(n), ⊙), which preserves the invariant metric h on (su(n), ⊙).
Proof. This follows from Lemma 5.3, relating the automorphism groups of (su(n), ◦, h) and its unitalization
and the fact (see [85]) that the automorphism group of (Herm(n, C), ⋆) is generated by the conjugation action
of U (n) and the outer automorphism x → x̄.                                                                 
Remark 9.12. The Ad(SU (n))-invariance of ⊙ is equivalent to the statement that ad(X) is a derivation of
⊙ for X ∈ su(n). That is,
(9.11)                              [X, Y ⊙ Z] = [X, Y ] ⊙ Z + Y ⊙ [X, Z].

   The decomposition of (su(n), ⊙) into the ±1 eigenspaces su(n)± (the subspaces comprising the imaginary
skew-Hermitian and the real antisymmetric matrices) of the outer automorphism Θ is an h-orthogonal
decomposition that makes (su(n), ⊙) a Z/2Z-graded algebra. The outer automorphism Θ corresponds to the
outer autormorphism θ of (Herm0 (n, C), ◦) given by θ(x) = x̄, and the +1 eigenspace of θ is isomorphic to
(Herm0 (n, R), ◦)). In this setting, Lemma 9.7 admits the following reformulation.
Lemma 9.13. A Cartan subalgebra B of the Lie algebra (su(n), [ · , · ]) is a subalgebra of (su(n), ⊙) isomor-
phic to En−1 (R). Moreover the Weyl group of su(n) acts as the automorphism group of (B, ⊙).
Proof. Since any two Cartan subalgebras are unitarily conjugate and the multiplication ⊙ is unitarily in-
variant, it suffices to prove the claims for the subspace B of (su(n), ⊙) comprising diagonal matrices. It is
apparent from (9.10) that B is a subalgebra of (su(n), ⊙) on which the Weyl group acts by automorphisms.
Since B is an abelian Lie subalgebra of su(n), it follows from (9.12) that (B, ⊙) is conformally associative.
The proof of the final claim can be based on this observation and the uniqueness statement in Theorem 6.8.
Alternatively, it follows from Lemma 9.7.                                                                  

   Corollary 9.19 bounds the sectional nonassociativity of (Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h). It follows from Lemma 9.14,
that bounds the sectional nonassociativity of (Herm(n, k), ⋆, ĥ). The proof uses the extension to real Hurwitz
algebras of the Chern-do Carmo-Kobayashi-Böttcher-Wenzel inequality reported as Theorem A.2 in the
appendix.
Lemma 9.14. Let k be a real Hurwitz algebra. Equip (Herm(n, k), B) with the metric ĥ(x, y) = N −1 tr L⋆ (x⋆
y) = n−1 tr(x ⋆ y) where N = dim Herm(n, k) and n is the rank of Herm(n, k). For linearly independent

    (1) If k is associative, equality holds in the lower bound of (9.13) if and only if x and y commute with
        respect to the matrix product; in particular, if x and x⋆x are linearly independent, then K(x, x⋆x) = 0.
    (2) Equality holds in the upper bound of (9.13) if and only if x and y are simultaneously equivalent under
        Aut(Herm(n, k), ⋆) to scalar multiples of e11 − enn and e1n + en1 .

Remark 9.15. In [55, Table 5.1], X. Liu gives sharp upper bounds on the sectional curvatures of irreducible
Riemannian symmetric spaces. It would be interesting to know if the upper bound of (9.13) can be deduced
from Liu’s result for the relevant symmetric spaces (types AI, AII, and EIV). In this regard [8] is relevant. ⊳
Lemma 9.16. If a Euclidean metrized commutative algebra (A, ◦, h) has nonnegative sectional nonassocia-
tivity, then it is not exact and Π⊥ (e) ⊂ [0, 1/2] for any minimal idempotent e ∈ M(A, ◦, h).
Proof. By (2a) of Lemma 2.13 there exists 0 6= e ∈ I(A, ◦). Were (A, ◦) exact, then, because tr L◦ (e) = 0
and 1 is an eigenvalue of L◦ (e), there would be a negative eigenvalue of L◦ (e). Let z ∈ A be h-orthogonal
to e ∈ M(A, ◦, h) and suppose L◦ (e)z = λz. Because the sectional nonassociativity is nonnegative, 0 ≤
h(e ◦ e, z ◦ z) − |e ◦ z|2h = λ(1 − λ)|z|2h , so λ ∈ [0, 1/2] (the upper bound by Lemma 2.13).           
Example 9.17. Together Lemmas 9.14 and 9.16 show that min Π⊥ (e) ≥ 0 for e ∈ M(Herm(n, k), ⋆, h).                          ⊳
Lemma 9.18. Over a base field of characteristic zero, if the unitalization of the metrized commutative algebra
(A, ◦, h) is power associative, then, for any 0 6= x ∈ A, either x generates a one-dimensional subalgebra of
(A, ◦) or K(x ◦ x, x) = −1.
                   COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                       37


Proof. By Lemma 9.1, if the unitalization of (A, ◦, h) is power associative, then [x ◦ x, x, x] = |x|2h x ◦ x −
h(x ◦ x, x)x for all x ∈ A. The quantity |x|2h x ◦ x − h(x ◦ x, x)x vanishes if and only if x ◦ x is a multiple of
x, in which case |x|2h x ◦ x = h(x ◦ x, x)x and x generates a one-dimensional subalgebra of (A, ◦). Otherwise,
pairing this quantity with x ◦ x and comparing with (7.1) yields

(9.21)                                  −K(x, x ◦ x) = |x|2h([x◦x,x,x],x◦x)
                                                            |x◦x|2 −h(x◦x,x)2
                                                                              = 1,
                                                               h       h


which completes the proof.                                                                                            

Corollary 9.19. Let k be a real Hurwitz algebra. The sectional nonassociativity of the deunitalization
(Herm0 (n, k), ◦, h) of a simple Euclidean Jordan algebra of rank n ≥ 2 (n = 3 if k = O) satisfies
(9.22)                                             −1 ≤ K(x, y) ≤ n−2
                                                                   2 .

and both bounds are attained. If k is associative, the associator is given by
(9.23)                              [x, y, z]◦ = 41 [y, [x, z]] − h(x, y)z + h(y, z)x,
for x, y, z ∈ Herm0 (n, k).
Proof. The bounds (9.22) are immediate from Lemma 9.14 and (7.7). By Lemma 9.18, if x ∈ Herm0 (n, k)
is not a multiple of an idempotent or a square-zero element, then K(x ◦ x, x) = −1, showing that the lower
bound in (9.22) is attained. That the upper bound in (9.22) is attained for x = e1n + en1 and y = e11 − enn
follows from computations using x ◦ x = e11 + enn − n2 I = y ◦ y and x ◦ y = 0. The identity (9.23) follows
from (6.7) and (9.14) when k is associative.                                                              

            10. The conformal extension of a Killing metrized commutative algebra
   An n-dimensional Killing metrized exact commutative k-algebra gives rise to one of (n + 1)-dimensions
when char k = 0 and k is algebraically closed or k = R. The restriction on the field is because square-roots
are taken in the construction. This construction is an algebraic version of [27, Lemma 6.9] and [26, Lemma
7.15], whose purely algebraic content is somewhat obscured because they are stated in terms of the associated
cubic polynomials.

Lemma 10.5. Let k be a field that is algebraically closed of characteristic zero or R.
  (1) If Ψ : (A1 , ◦1 ) → (A2 , ◦2 ) is an algebra homomorphism between Killing metrized exact commutative
      k-algebras, then Ψ̄ : A1 ⊕ k → A2 ⊕ k defined by Ψ̄(x, r) = (Ψ(x), r) is an algebra homomorphism
      between their conformal extensions.
  (2) For a Killing metrized exact commutative k-algebra (A, ◦), the map Ψ → Ψ̄ is an injective group
      homomorphism Aut(A, ◦) → Aut(Ā, ¯◦).
Proof. Because Ψ is an algebra homomorphism, it preserves Killing forms. A computation using this obser-
vation shows that Ψ̄ is an algebra homomorphim. Claim (2) follows from (1).                          
Corollary 10.6. For a field k that is algebraically closed of characteristic zero or equal to R, the conformal
extension of En (k) is isomorphic to En+1 (k).
Proof. By Lemma 10.1, the conformal extension of En (k) is a conformally associative Killing metrized exact
commutative k-algebra with Killing form that is positive definite when k = R. By Theorem 6.8, such an
algebra is isomorphic to En+1 (k).                                                                       
Remark 10.7. By Lemma 10.1, the conformal extension of a Killing metrized exact commutative algebra
that is not conformally associative is not conformally associative, so any such example yields examples in
all higher dimensions. The construction of such algebras in [26] was the author’s original motivation for this
entire project.                                                                                              ⊳
40                                                   DANIEL J. F. FOX

Lemma 10.10. Let (A, ◦, h) be a Euclidean metrized exact commutative algebra of dimension n ≥ 2. If
e ∈ I(A, ◦), then τ◦ (e, e) ≥ n/(n−1), with equality if and only if Π⊥ (e) comprises −1/(n−1) with multiplicity
n − 1.
Proof. Because L◦ (e) is h-self-adjoint, it is diagonalizable with eigenvalues 1 and λ1 , . . . , λn−1 ∈ R. Because
tr L◦ (e) = 0 and L◦ (e) preserves hei⊥ , the λ1 , . . . , λn−1 equal the eigenvalues of the restriction of L◦ (e) to
                                                                                             Pn−1
hei⊥ and sum to −1. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, τ◦ (e, e) = tr L◦ (e)2 = 1 + i=1 λ2i ≥ n/(n − 1),
with equality if and only if λi = −1/(n − 1) for all 1 ≤ i ≤ n − 1.                                                

An element w̄ = (w, r) ∈ Ā is square-zero if and only if w ◦ w = 2cn rw and nr2 = |w|2 . Because nr2 = |w|2 ,
if w̄ 6= 0, then r 6= 0 and w 6= 0. In this case 2c1n r w is idempotent in (A, ◦). Since if w̄ is square-zero,
any multiple λw̄ is also square-zero, it can be supposed without loss of generality that 2cn r = 1 and w is
idempotent. In this case |w|2 = nr2 = 4−1 nc−2   n = (n + 2)n(n − 1)/4. This proves (3).

Corollary 10.14. The conformal extension of a Euclidean Killing metrized exact commutative algebra (A, ◦)
is spanned by minimal idempotents if and only if A is spanned by minimal idempotents.
Proof. If M(A, ◦, τ◦ ) spans A, by Lemma 10.13, the set {(0, − nc1n )} ∪ {ē− : e ∈ M(A, ◦, τ◦ )} is contained
in M(Ā, ¯◦, τ¯◦ ) and spans (Ā, ¯
                                  ◦, τ¯◦ ). Conversely, if (Ā, ¯◦, τ¯◦ ) is spanned by minimal idempotents, by Lemma
10.13 these include w̄ and at least n more linearly independent minimal idempotents of the form ēi − for
ei ∈ M(A, ◦, τ◦ ) satisfying τ◦ (ei , ei ) = n/n − 1. The e1 , . . . , en span A.                                   

                                 11. Triple algebras and Nahm algebras
   This section describes an iterative construction yielding simple Killing metrized exact commutative al-
gebras of arbitrarily high dimension. Given a commutative algebra (A, ◦), the construction produces a
commutative algebra structure on the triple T(A) = A ⊕ A ⊕ A. This iterative construction preserves both
the Killing metrized condition and simplicity, so yields simple Killing metrized exact commutative algebras
of arbitrarily high dimension. A preliminary version of this material appeared in [26, Section 7.2].
   Lemma 11.3 shows that the triple algebra is simply the tensor product with E3 . Among tensor products
with En what is special about the exponent n = 3 is that the polarization Q(x, y, z) = P (x + y + z) − P (x +
y) − P (y + z) − P (z + x) + P (x) + P (y) + P (z) of the cubic polynomial P (x) of a metrized commutative
algebra (A, ◦, h) can be viewed as a cubic polynomial on A ⊕ A ⊕ A, so defines on A ⊕ A ⊕ A (equipped with
the direct sum metric) a structure of a metrized commutative algebra. See (11.13) of Lemma 11.9.
44                                                       DANIEL J. F. FOX


Definition 11.1. The triple (T(A), ⊙) of the algebra (A, ◦) is the exact commutative algebra defined as
follows. Let T(A) = A ⊕ A ⊕ A, let πi : T(A) → A be projection onto the ith component, and write xi = πi (x).
Write νi : A → T(A) for the inclusion as the ith component. The notations x = ν1 (x) + ν2 (x) + ν3 (x) and
x = (x1 , x2 , x3 ) are synonymous, and which is used depends on context. Define a commutative multiplication
⊙ on T(A) by
                 x ⊙ y = 12 (ν1 (x2 ◦ y3 + y2 ◦ x3 ) + ν2 (x3 ◦ y1 + y3 ◦ x1 ) + ν3 (1 ◦ y2 + y1 ◦ x2 ))
(11.1)
                       = 12 (x2 ◦ y3 + y2 ◦ x3 , x3 ◦ y1 + y3 ◦ x1 , x1 ◦ y2 + y1 ◦ x2 ) .
Alternatively, the left multiplication L⊙ (x) of T(A) has the block matrix form
                                                                            
                                                     0     R◦ (x3 ) L◦ (x2 )
(11.2)                              L⊙ (x) = 21  L◦ (x3 )    0     R◦ (x1 ) ,
                                                  R◦ (x2 ) L◦ (x1 )    0
where L◦ , R◦ : A → End(A) are the left and right regular representations of (A, ◦).
  One reason for choosing the factor of 1/2 in (11.1) is that when (A, ◦) is commutative the injective diagonal
map ∆ : A → T(A) defined by ∆(x) = ν1 (x) + ν2 (x) + ν3 (x) is an algebra homomorphism.
Example 11.2. Regard k as a Killing metrized commutative algebra. The Killing form of k is τR (x, x) = x2
and the associated cubic polynomial is 16 x3 . The triple T(k) has multiplication table (6.19) with c = 1/2,
so by Example 6.9 is isomorphic to E3 (k). This provides motivation for Lemma 11.3. The trace-form τT(k)
equals 21 (x1 y1 + x2 y2 + x3 y3 ) for x, y ∈ k3 , in accord with (11.12) below, and the cubic polynomial of
(T(k), ◦, τT(k) ) is 41 x1 x2 x3 , in accord with (11.13) below.                                           ⊳
   The Lie algebra so(3) is defined over a ground field k of characteristic not equal to 2 as a 3-dimensional
k-vector space V equipped with a basis {e1 , e2 , e3 } and the Lie bracket satisfying [e1 , e2 ] = e3 and its
cyclic permutations. With respect to its Killing form B, the basis {e1 , e2 , e3 } is orthogonal and satisfies
B(ei , ei ) = −2 for 1 ≤ i ≤ 3. The special orthogonal group SO(3) is defined to be the group of invertible
                                                              V3
linear automorphisms preserving B and fixing e1 ∧ e2 ∧ e3 ∈      V.
Lemma 11.3.
     (1) If (g, [ · , · ]) is an anticommutative algebra, then (T(g), ⊙) is isomorphic to so(3) ⊗k g.
     (2) If (A, ◦) is a commutative algebra, then (T(A), ⊙) is isomorphic to E3 ⊗k A.
Proof. When (g, [ · , · ]) is anticommutative, so that ad = L[ · , · ] = −R[ · , · ] , (11.2) becomes
                                                                                 
                                                   0      − ad(x3 ) ad(x2 )
(11.3)                            L⊙ (x) = 21  ad(x3 )      0         − ad(x1 ) ,
                                                − ad(x2 ) ad(x1 )         0
so that T(g) carries the commutative multiplication
(11.4)                  x ⊙ y = 12 ([x2 , y3 ] + [y2 , x3 ], [x3 , y1 ] + [y3 , x1 ], [x1 , y2 ] + [y1 , x2 ]) .
There is a basis {e1 , e2 , e3 } of so(3) that satisfies [e1 , e2 ] = e3 and its cyclic permutations. The map
Ψ : so(3) ⊗k g → T(g) defined by Ψ((a1 e1 + a2 e2 + a3 e3 ) ⊗ x) = 2(a1 x, a2 x, a3 x) is the desired isomorphism.
When (A, ◦) is commutative, (11.2) becomes
                                                                              
                                                      0     L◦ (x3 ) L◦ (x2 )
(11.5)                               L⊙ (x) = 21 L◦ (x3 )      0      L◦ (x1 ) .
                                                   L◦ (x2 ) L◦ (x1 )      0
By Example 6.9 there is a basis {e1 , e2 , e3 } of E3 (k) that satisfies e1 ◦ e1 = 0, e1 ◦ e2 = e3 and their cyclic
permutations. The map Ψ : E3 ⊗k A → T(A) defined by Ψ((a1 e1 + a2 e2 + a3 e3 ) ⊗ x) = 2(a1 x, a2 x, a3 x) is the
desired isomorphism.                                                                                             
                 COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                          45


   When (g, [ · , · ]) is a Lie algebra, the algebra (Nahm(g) = T(g), ⊙) is the Nahm algebra of (g, [ · , · ])
defined by M. Kinyon and A. Sagle in [48]. This observation provides additional motivation for studying
triple algebras. As will become apparent, some of the results about Nahm algebras shown in [48] carry
over to the triple algebras of commutative algebras, although the precise claims and their proofs usually
require modification. On the other hand, that the Nahm algebra of a Lie algebra and the triple algebra of
a commutative algebra are specializations of a single construction is somewhat misleading, as the following
alternative construction of the triple algebra of a commutative algebra suggests.
   Let (A4 = A ⊕ A ⊕ A ⊕ A, ◦) be the direct sum of 4 copies of the algebra (A, ◦). The symmetric group
S4 acts freely on A4 by permuting the components, and this action is by automorphisms of (A4 , ◦). Let
V = {z ∈ A4 : z0 + z1 + z2 + z3 = 0} where zi is the projection of z ∈ A4 on the component i, and the
components are indexed by {0, 1, 2, 3} and let ∆(A) = {(z, z, z, z) ∈ A4 : z ∈ A} ⊂ A4 be the S4 -invariant
diagonal subspace. The projection Π ∈ End(A4 ) onto V along ∆(A) is S4 -equivariant. The linear map
φ : T(A) → A4 defined by
(11.6)               φ(x) = 12 (x1 + x2 + x3 , x1 − x2 − x3 , −x1 + x2 − x3 , −x1 − x2 + x3 )

is injective, with image equal to V (this step requires that char k 6= 2). A computation shows that
                                                  1
                                                                                                               
(11.7)       φ(x ⊙ y) − Π(φ(x) ◦ φ(y)) = φ        2 (x3 ◦ y2 − y2 ◦ x3 , x1 ◦ y3 − y3 ◦ x1 , x2 ◦ y1 − y1 ◦ x2 )   .

In particular, if (A, ◦) is commutative, then
(11.8)                                        Π(φ(x) ◦ φ(y)) = φ(x ⊙ y)

for x, y ∈ T(A). For commutative (A, ◦), (11.8) could be taken as the definition of ⊙.
    Lemma 11.4, that shows that there are actions of SO(3) and the symmetric group S4 by outer automor-
phisms of (T(A), ⊙), when (A, ◦) is, respectively, Lie or commutative.
    The action of S4 on T(A) is induced from the action of S4 on A4 via φ, by φ(σ · x) = σ · φ(x) for x ∈ T(A)
and σ ∈ S4 . Consider the group Γ acting on T(A) by permutation of the summands (so as S3 ) and changes
of signs of an even number (so 0 or 2) of components. Let (ij) be the permutation of i, j ∈ {1, 2, 3} and let
(±, ±, ±) indicate the sign change on the components of T(A). It follows from (11.6) that (ij) corresponds
to the permutation (ij) of A ⊕ A ⊕ A ⊕ A, while (+, −, −), (−, +, −), and (−, −, +) correspond respectively
to the permutations (01)(23), (02)(13) and (03)(12) of {0, 1, 2, 3}. Since the product of (01)(23) with (23) is
(01) and similarly cycling the the indices, the image of Γ contains all the transpositions in S4 , so generates
S4 . As Γ has the same order as S4 , it is isomorphic to S4 , and moreover, the preceding shows that its
action is the action induced via φ defined previously. More explicitly, if (ij) denotes a transposition, and
x = (x1 , x2 , x3 ) ∈ T(A),
              (01) · x = (x1 , −x3 , −x2 ),   (02) · x = (−x3 , x2 , −x1 ), (03) · x = (−x2 , −x1 , x3 ),
(11.9)
              (12) · x = (x2 , x1 , x3 ),     (13) · x = (x3 , x2 , x1 ),    (23) · x = (x1 , x3 , x2 ).

Lemma 11.4. Let char k = 0 and let (A, ◦) and (B, ◦) be k-algebras.
                                                                            P
    (1) If Ψ ∈ hom(A, B) is an algebra homomorphism then ∆(Ψ)(x) = 3i=1 νi (Ψ(xi )) is an algebra homo-
        morphism from (T(A), ⊙) to (T(B), ⊙). In particular, the map ∆ : Aut(A, ◦) → Aut(T(A), ⊙) is an
        injective group homomorphism.
    (2) If (A, ◦) = (g, [ · , · ]) is a Lie algebra, the action of SO(3) on (Nahm(g), ⊙) defined by νi (r · x) =
        Ri j νj (x) where r = Ri j ∈ SO(3) and x ∈ Nahm(g) is an action by automorphisms.
    (3) If (A, ◦) is commutative, the action of S4 on (T(A), ⊙) defined by φ(σ · x) = σ · φ(x) for x ∈ T(A)
        and σ ∈ S4 , where φ is as in (11.6), is an action by automorphisms.
Proof. The only part of (1) that requires comment is the injectivity of ∆. If x ∈ A, then, by definition,
∆(Ψ)(∆(x)) = ∆(Ψ(x)). Hence if ∆(Ψ) is the identity, then ∆(x) = ∆(Ψ(x)) for all x ∈ A, and this shows Ψ
is the identity. Claim (2) is [48, Corollary 9.1]. Claim (3) follows from (11.8), the definition of the S4 action
on T(A), that S4 acts on (A4 , ◦) by automorphisms, and the S4 -equivariance of the projection Π. Explicitly,
46                                                        DANIEL J. F. FOX


for σ ∈ S4 and x, y, ∈ T(A),
                 φ(σ · x ⊙ σ · y) = Π(φ(σ · x) ◦ φ(σ · y)) = Π(σ · φ(x) ◦ σ · φ(y)) = Π(σ · (φ(x) ◦ φ(y))
(11.10)
                                   = σ · Π(φ(x) ◦ φ(y) = σ · φ(x ⊙ y) = φ(σ · (x ⊙ y)),
and by the injectivity of φ this shows σ · x ⊙ σ · y = σ · (x ⊙ y).                                                  
Remark 11.5. Lemma 11.4 should be compared with the discussion in [44, Chapter 5.2].                        ⊳
Remark 11.6. It follows from Lemma 11.4 that a derivation D of (A, ◦) extends to a derivation T(D) of
(T(A), ⊙) defined by νi (T(D)x) = Dxi for x ∈ T(A). If (g, [ · , · ]) is a Lie algebra, Nahm(g) is also a Lie
algebra, with the componentwise Lie bracket. For z ∈ g the derivation ad(z) of g extends to a derivation
T(ad(z)) of T(A), that is given by the componentwise Lie bracket with the diagonal image ∆(z), so that
T(adg (z)) = adT(g) (∆(z)). Equivalently,
(11.11)                                [∆(z), x ⊙ y] = [∆(z), x] ⊙ y + x ⊙ [∆(z), y]
for x, y ∈ Nahm(g) and z ∈ g.                                                                                        ⊳
   For a commutative algebra (A, ◦) and a Lie algebra (g, [ · , · ]), there follow from (11.5) and (11.3)

The second equality of (11.12) is [48, Theorem 7.2]. From (11.12) and the invariance of the Killing form it
follows that τNahm(g) is invariant [48, Theorem 7.4]. By [48, Theorem 5.1], Nahm(g) is simple if and only if
g is simple, and by [48, Theorem 5.3], Nahm(g) is a direct sum of simple ideals if and only if g is semisimple.
From these and the preceding remarks there follows:
Theorem 11.7 ([48, Corollary 7.7]). For a real Lie algebra g the following are equivalent.
   (1) g is semisimple.
   (2) The Killing form Bg is nondegenerate.
   (3) Nahm(g) is a direct sum of simple ideals.
   (4) τNahm(g) is nondegenerate.
Corollary 11.8. If g is a semisimple (simple) real Lie algebra, then (Nahm(g), ◦, τ ) is a semisimple (simple)
Killing metrized exact commutative algebra.
   The formally analogous results for the triple algebra of a commutative algebra (A, ◦) are also valid, with
the changes that the invariance of the trace form of (A, ◦) is no longer automatic, and has to be introduced
as a hypothesis, and that the proofs follow different lines.
Lemma 11.9. Let char k = 0 and let (T(A), ⊙) be the triple of the commutative k-algebra (A, ◦).
  (1) τ⊙ is invariant if and only if τ◦ is invariant. In this case, the cubic polynomials P⊙ and P◦ of
      (T(A), ⊙, τ⊙ ) and (A, ◦, τ◦ ) are related by
               4P⊙ (x) = τ◦ (x1 ◦ x2 , x3 ) = P◦ (x1 + x2 + x3 ) − P◦ (x1 + x2 ) − P◦ (x1 + x3 ) − P◦ (x2 + x3 )
(11.13)
                                                    + P◦ (x1 ) + P◦ (x2 ) + P◦ (x3 ).
         In particular 2P⊙ (∆(x)) = 3P◦ (x) for x ∈ A.
     (2) τ⊙ is nondegenerate if and only if τ◦ is nondegenerate.
     (3) If k = R, τ⊙ is positive definite if and only if τ◦ is positive definite.
Proof. That the invariance of τ◦ implies the invariance of τ⊙ is immediate from (11.1) and (11.12). By
(11.12), 2τ⊙ (∆(x), ∆(y)) = 3τ◦ (x, y). The invariance of τ⊙ implies that of τ◦ follows from this and the fact
that ∆ is a homomorphism. By (11.12) and the invariance of τ◦ ,
                  12P⊙ (x) = 2τ⊙ (x ⊙ x, x) = τ◦ (x2 ◦ x3 , x1 ) + τ◦ (x3 ◦ x1 , x2 ) + τ◦ (x1 ◦ x2 , x3 )
(11.14)
                            = 3τ◦ (x1 ◦ x2 , x3 ).
                   COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                       47


The second equality of (11.13) is true in general. This shows (1). If x ∈ T(A) satisfies τ⊙ (x, y) = 0 for all
                                         P3                    P3
y ∈ T(A), then, by (11.12), 0 = 2τ⊙ (x, i=1 αi νi (v)) = τ◦ ( i=1 αi xi , v) = 0 for all v ∈ A and αi ∈ k. If τ◦ is
nondegenerate, this implies all linear combinations of x1 , x2 , and x3 vanish, so x1 = x2 = x3 = 0, which shows
x = 0. If x ∈ A is such that τ◦ (x, y) = 0 for all y ∈ A, then τ⊙ (∆(x), y) = τ◦ (x, y1 ) + τ◦ (x, y2 ) + τ◦ (x, y3 ) = 0
for all y ∈ T(A). If τ⊙ is nondegenerate, this implies ∆(x) = 0, so x = 0, because ∆ is injective. This proves
(2). Suppose k = R. From 2τ⊙ (ν1 (x), ν1 (x)) = τ◦ (x, x) for x ∈ A, it follows that τ◦ is positive definite if τ⊙ is
                                            P3
positive definite, while from 2τ⊙ (x, x) = i=1 τ◦ (xi , xi ) for x ∈ T(A) there follows the converse. This shows
(3).                                                                                                                   

Theorem 11.10. Let char k = 0. The triple algebra (T(A), ⊙, τ⊙ ) of a commutative k-algebra (A, ◦) is
Killing metrized if and only if (A, ◦) is Killing metrized.
Proof. This follows from (1) and (2) of Lemma 11.9.                                                                   

Lemma 11.11. Let (T(A), ⊙) be the triple of the k-algebra (A, ◦) where char k 6= 2. Let J be an ideal in
T(A) and define Ji = πi (J) ⊂ A. Then J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 and J1 + J2 + J3 are ideals in (A, ◦).
Proof. Let a ∈ A and x ∈ J. By definition 2ν1 (x2 ◦a)+ν2(a◦x1 ) = ν3 (a)⊙x ∈ J, so x2 ◦a = 2π1 (ν3 (a)⊙x) ∈ J1
and a ◦ x1 = 2π2 (ν3 (a) ⊙ x) ∈ J2 . These relations remain valid under cyclic permutations of the indices. This
shows L◦ (A)J1 ⊂ J2 , L◦ (A)J2 ⊂ J3 , L◦ (A)J3 ⊂ J1 , R◦ (A)J2 ⊂ J1 , R◦ (A)J3 ⊂ J2 , and R◦ (A)J1 ⊂ J3 . Hence
L◦ (A)(J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 ) ⊂ J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 , R◦ (A)(J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 ) ⊂ J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 , L◦ (A)(J1 + J2 + J3 ) ⊂ J1 + J2 + J3 ,
and R◦ (A)(J1 + J2 + J3 ) ⊂ J1 + J2 + J3 , showing that J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 and J1 + J2 + J3 are two-sided ideals of
(A, ◦).                                                                                                          

   The proof of Theorem 11.12 is motivated by the proof of [48, Theorem 5.1], but even in the antisymmetric
case the argument is slightly stronger, as it makes no use of the Jacobi identity.
Theorem 11.12. If an k-algebra (A, ◦), where char k 6= 2, is either commutative or anticommutative, then
(T(A), ⊙) is simple if and only if (A, ◦) is simple.
Proof. Let J be an ideal in T(A) and define Ji = πi (J) ⊂ A. Since R◦ = ±L◦ , the proof of Lemma 11.11
shows L◦ (A)J1 ⊂ J2 ∩ J3 and the similar inclusions obtained by permuting the indices cyclically. There
follows A ◦ (A ◦ J1 ) ⊂ A ◦ (J2 ∩ J3 ) ⊂ (J1 ∩ J3 ) ∩ (J1 ∩ J2 ) = J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 , and similarly with J2 or J3 in place
of J1 . If A is simple then, by Lemma 11.11, either J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 = {0} or J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 = A. In the latter case,
J = T(A), so suppose J1 ∩ J2 ∩ J3 = {0}. Then if x, y ∈ A and a ∈ Ji there holds x ◦ (y ◦ a) = 0. Since
A is simple, A ◦ A = A. The set of z ∈ A such that z ◦ u = 0 for all u ∈ A is an ideal. Were it equal to A,
then the multiplication in A would be trivial, so this set is {0}. Hence x ◦ (y ◦ a) = 0 for all x, y ∈ A implies
y ◦ a = 0 for all y ∈ A, which in turn implies a = 0. Hence J1 = J2 = J3 = {0}, and so J = {0}. This
shows that if A is simple, then T(A) is simple. Now suppose T(A) is simple and let J be an ideal in A. Then
ν1 (J) + ν2 (J) + ν3 (J) = {(x, y, z) ∈ T(A) : x, y, z ∈ J} is an ideal in T(A). If it equals T(A) then J = A,
while if it equals {0} then J = {0}, so A is simple.                                                                

Remark 11.13. Because, by Example (11.2), T(k) is isomorphic to E3 (k), Theorems 11.10 and 11.12 give
an alternative proof that E3 (k) is a simple Killing metrized exact commutative algebra.                      ⊳
                                            3
Example 11.14. The triple algebra T(E (k)) is a simple 9-dimensional Killing metrized exact commutative
algebra. Let γ0 , γ1 , γ2 , γ3 be the generators of E3 (k) as in (3.10). The vectors {e1 = −(γ2 + γ3 ), e2 =
−(γ1 + γ3 ), e3 = −(γ1 + γ2 )} are τ◦ -orthogonal, satisfy τ◦ (ei , ei ) = 2, and satisfy (6.19) with c = 1. By

Lemma 11.16 (M. Kinyon - A. Sagle; [48]). For a finite-dimensional real Lie algebra g a nonzero element
x ∈ Nahm(g) is idempotent if and only if the components xi = πi (x) ∈ g of x generate a subalgebra of g
isomorphic to so(3).
Proof. That x be idempotent implies x1 = [x2 , x3 ], x2 = [x3 , x1 ], and x3 = [x1 , x2 ]. As is shown at the end
of section 4 of [48] (and is easy to check), these relations cannot be satisfied unless x1 ∧ x2 ∧ x3 6= 0, in which
case the xi generate a subalgebra of g isomorphic to so(3).                                                      
Example 11.17. Let e1 , e2 , e3 be the standard basis of so(3) such that e1 × e2 = e3 , e2 × e3 = e1 and
e3 × e1 = e2 . For an orientation preserving rotation g of R3 let gi = gei and note that g1 × g2 = g3 , etc. Then
Xg = ν1 (g1 ) + ν2 (g2 ) + ν3 (g3 ) ∈ Nahm(g) is idempotent. Let X0 be Xg for g the identity transformation. It
is straightforward to check that
(11.19)                  −2X0 ⊙ y = (−y5 − y9 , y4 , y7 , y2 , −y1 − y9 , y8 , y3 , y6 , −y1 − y5 ).
The orthocomplement of X0 is given by y1 + y5 + y9 = 0. On this orthocomplement, the eigenvalues of
L⊙ (X0 ) are 1/2 with 3-dimensional eigenspace spanned by the eigenvectors ν1 (e2 ) − ν2 (e1 ), ν1 (e3 ) − ν3 (e1 ),
and ν2 (e3 ) − ν3 (e2 ), and −1/2, with 5-dimensional eigenspace spanned by the eigenvectors ν1 (e1 ) − ν3 (e3 ),
ν2 (e2 ) − ν3 (e3 ), ν1 (e2 ) + ν2 (e1 ), ν1 (e3 ) + ν3 (e1 ), and ν2 (e3 ) + ν3 (e2 ).
    For g = so(3), any triple {x1 , x2 , x3 } of elements as in the proof of Lemma 11.16 is the image of the
standard triple {e1 , e2 , e3 } under an element of SO(3). It follows that every idempotent of Nahm(so(3)) has
the form Xg . Since all the idempotents have the same norm, and by (2a) of Lemma 2.13 there is necessarily
a minimal idempotent, all the Xg are minimal idempotents. However as the eigenvalues of L⊙ (Xg ) are the
same as those of L⊙ (X0 ), and among them 1/2 occurs with multiplicity 3, no Xg has orthogonal spectrum
contained in (−∞, 0], and Nahm(so(3)) has no minimal idempotent with orthogonal spectrum contained in
(−∞, 0].
    Let fi = 2νi (ei ) where {e1 , e2 , e3 } is the standard orthonormal basis of so(3). The fi satisfy the relations
(6.19) (with c = 1) defining the algebra E3 , showing that Nahm(so(3)) contains E3 as a subalgebra. This is
observed, albeit not quite in these terms, in [48, example 5.2].                                                    ⊳
Theorem 11.18. For a compact simple real Lie algebra, g, the Nahm algebra (Nahm(g), ◦, τ ) is a simple
Killing metrized exact commutative algebra that has sectional nonassociativities of both signs. In particular
it is not isomorphic to a direct sum of real simplicial algebras.
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                                  49


Proof. Let (g, [ · , · ]) be a real Lie algebra. The subspace ∆(g) = {x ∈ Nahm(g) : ν1 (x) = ν2 (x) =
ν3 (x)} is a ◦-trivial subalgebra. Its τ◦ -orthocomplement ∆(g)⊥ comprises those x ∈ Nahm(g) for which
ν1 (x)+ν2 (x)+ν3 (x) = 0. These satisfy ∆(g)◦∆(g) = {0}, ∆(g)◦∆(g)⊥ ⊂ ∆(g)⊥ , and ∆(g)⊥ ◦∆(g)⊥ ⊂ ∆(g).
Since (∆(g), ◦) is an associative subalgebra of Nahm(g), T◦ (x, y) = 0 for x, y ∈ ∆(g). Straightforward
calculations using (11.12) and the invariance of Bg yield
                 τ◦ (x ◦ x, y ◦ y) − τ◦ (x ◦ y, x ◦ y)
                                                                                                                           
(11.20)           = 12 Cycle{1,2,3} −Bg ([x1 , x2 ], [y1 , y2 ]) + 41 Bg ([x1 , y2 ] + [y1 , x2 ], [x1 , y2 ] + [y1 , x2 ])
                                                                                                                           
                  = 12 Cycle{1,2,3} −Bg ([x1 , y1 ], [x2 , y2 ]) + 41 Bg ([x1 , y2 ] − [y1 , x2 ], [x1 , y2 ] − [y1 , x2 ]) ,
in which Cycle{1,2,3} indicates the sum over the cyclic permutations of the indicated indices. Suppose g
is simple and compact, so that Bg is negative definite. If x ∈ ∆(g), then the first term on the second
line of (11.20) vanishes and so, as the second term on the second line of (11.20) is nonpositive, it follows
that T◦ (x, y) ≤ 0 whenever x ∈ ∆(g). Since g is simple there are a and b in g such that [a, b] 6= 0. Let
x = ν1 (a) + ν2 (b), y = ν1 (b) − ν2 (a), and z = ν1 (a) − ν2 (b). Since νi (a) ◦ νi (a) = 0 for i = 1, 2, 3,
and 2ν1 (a) ◦ ν2 (b) = ν3 ([a, b]) there hold x ◦ y = 0, x ◦ z = 0, and x ◦ x = y ◦ y = −z ◦ z. Hence
τ◦ (x◦x, y◦y)−τ◦ (x◦y, x◦y) = − 21 Bg ([a, b], [a, b]) > 0, and τ◦ (x◦x, z◦z)−τ◦ (x◦z, x◦z) = 21 Bg ([a, b], [a, b]) < 0,
the inequalities because g is compact. This shows that T◦ (x, y) > 0 and T◦ (x, z) < 0, so (Nahm(g), ◦, τ )
has sectional nonassociativities of both signs. By Lemma 8.1, a direct sum of real simplicial algebras has
nonpositive sectional nonassociativity, so (Nahm(g), ◦, τ ) is not isomorphic to any such direct sum.                 

   What follows shows how conclusions like those of Theorem 11.18 can be obtained for triple algebras. Let
(T(A), ⊙) be the triple algebra of the algebra (A, ◦). For i ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3} define linear maps Γi : A → T(A) by
(11.21)      Γ0 (x) = ∆(x),         Γ1 (x) = (x, −x, −x),             Γ2 (x) = (−x, x, −x),            Γ3 (x) = (−x, −x, x),

and note Γ0 (x)+Γ1 (x)+Γ2 (x)+Γ3 (x) = 0. From (11.9) it follows that, for i ∈ {1, 2, 3}, Γi is the composition
of an element of S4 with Γ0 . For example Γ1 = (01) · Γ0 . Using that S4 acts by algebra automorphisms, it
is straightforward to check the relations
                Γi (x) ⊙ Γi (y) = 12 Γi (x ◦ y + y ◦ x),
(11.22)         Γi (x) ⊙ Γj (y) + Γj (x) ⊙ Γi (y) = − 21 (Γi (x ◦ y + y ◦ x) + Γj (x ◦ y + y ◦ x)) ,
                Γi (x) ⊙ Γj (y) − Γj (x) ⊙ Γi (y) = sgn(ijkl)
                                                        2     (Γk (x ◦ y − y ◦ x) − Γl (x ◦ y − y ◦ x))

for x, y ∈ A, where {i, j, k, l} is a permutation of {0, 1, 2, 3} and sgn(ijkl) is ±1 as it is an even or odd
permutation. The first identity of (11.22) shows that Γi (A) is a subalgebra of (T(A), ⊙) that is trivial if ◦ is
antisymmetric.
   Let ∇(A) = {x ∈ T(A) : x1 + x2 + x3 = 0}. For i, j ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3} define linear maps ∇ij : A → ∇(A) ⊂ T(A)
by ∇ij = 12 (Γi − Γj ) = −∇ji . For example ∇23 (x) = 21 (Γ2 (x) − Γ3 (x)) = (0, x, −x). Any two of ∇12 (x),
∇23 (x), ∇31 (x) constitute a basis of ∇(A). There hold

and the identities obtained from it by cyclically permuting the indices {1, 2, 3}. This shows that ∆(A) ⊙
∇(A) ⊂ ∇(A). If τ⊙ is nondegenerate then the orthogonal complement ∆(A)⊥ of ∆(A) equals ∇(A) and
∆(A) ◦ ∆(A)⊥ ⊂ ∆(A)⊥ .
  Now suppose (A, ◦) is commutative. In this case, the relations (11.22) and (11.23) simplify to
(11.24)           Γi (x) ⊙ Γi (y) = Γi (x ◦ y), Γi (x) ⊙ Γj (y) = − 21 (Γi (x ◦ y) + Γj (x ◦ y)) ,

for distinct i, j, k ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}. The first equation of (11.24) shows that Γi : A → T(A) is an injective algebra
homomorphism for all i ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}.

Theorem 11.19. Over a field k of characteristic 0, the triple algebra T(En (k)) ≃ E3 (k) ⊗ En (k) is a simple
Killing metrized exact commutative algebra that is not isomorphic to E3n (k).
Proof. That T(En (k)) is a simple Killing metrized exact commutative algebra follows from Theorems 11.12
and 11.10. The spectrum of a minimal idempotent e ∈ M(En (k)) contains only −1/(n − 1) with multiplicity
n − 1. Let y ∈ En (k) be an eigenvector with eigenvalue −1/(n − 1). By (11.28) and (11.27),
                                          1                            1

(11.31)                                  = ∆(e) ⊙ ∇i (y) + 12 ∆(e) ⊙ ∇i (e ◦ y) − 2(3n−1) τ◦ (e, e)∇i (y)

This shows T(En (k)) is not intrinsically projectively associative, so is not isomorphic to E3n (k).              
     Let (A, ◦) be a commutative algebra. For x ∈ A define a 3-dimensional subspace
(11.32)                                     E(x) = Span{Γi (x) : 0 ≤ i ≤ 3}
and a two-dimensional subspace
                      Z(x) = Span{∇ij (x) : i, j ∈ {1, 2, 3}} = Span{∇i (x) : i ∈ {1, 2, 3}}
(11.33)
                             = {(ax, bx, −(a + b)x) ∈ T(A) : a, b ∈ k}.
Suppose w ∈ Z(x) ∩ Z(y). Then there are a, b, c, d ∈ k such that (ax, bx, −(a + b)x) = w = (cy, dy, −(c + d)y),
and this can be so only if x and y are linearly dependent. Hence, if x, y ∈ A are linearly independent, then
Z(x) ∩ Z(y) = {0}.
Lemma 11.20. Let (T(A), ⊙) be the triple of the commutative k-algebra (A, ◦), where char k = 0.
  (1) If e ∈ I(A, ◦), then Γi (e) ∈ I(T(A), ⊙) for i ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3} and:
       (a) The subspace E(e) of (T(A), ⊙) is a subalgebra isomorphic to E3 (k).
       (b) For i ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}, −1/2 is an eigenvalue of L⊙ (Γi (e)) with multiplicity at least 2.
  (2) If, for x ∈ A, λ is an eigenvalue of L◦ (x) with multiplicity m, then −λ/2 is an eigenvalue of L⊙ (∆(x))
      with multiplicity at least 2m.
                 COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                 51


    (3) If z ∈ N(A, ◦), then ∇i (z) ∈ N(T(A), ⊙) for 1 ≤ i ≤ 3 and the subspace E(z) is a trivial subalgebra
        of (T(A), ⊙).

Proof. That, for e ∈ I(A, ◦), E(e) is a subalgebra of (T(A), ⊙) and, for z ∈ N(A, ◦), E(z) is a trivial subal-
gebra of (T(A), ⊙), are immediate from (11.24). From (11.24) it follows that Γi (e), i ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}, satisfy
the relations (3.10), so E(e) is isomorphic to E3 (k). By (11.25), Γi (e) ⊙ ∇jk (e) = − 12 ∇jk (e) for distinct
i, j, k ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}. This shows the two-dimensional subspace Z(e) is contained in the −1/2-eigenspace of
L⊙ (Γi (e)), so the multiplicity of −1/2 as an eigenvalue is at least 2. If L◦ (x)y = λy, then, by (11.25),
L⊙ (∆(x))∇ij (y) = −(λ/2)∇ij (y), so Z(y) is contained in the −1/2-eigenspace of L⊙ (∆(x)). Since the
subspaces Z(y) corresponding to linearly independent elements of the λ-eigenspace of L◦ (x) are pairwise
transverse, if λ is an eigenvalue of multiplicity m of L◦ (x), then −λ/2 is an eigenvalue of L⊙ (∆(x)) having
multiplicity at least 2m.                                                                                    

Lemma 11.21. Let (T(A), ⊙) be the triple of a Killing metrized commutative k-algebra (A, ◦), where char k =
0. The intrinsic sectional nonassociativities of (T(A), ◦, τ⊙ ) and (A, ◦, τ◦ ) are related by

(11.34)          T⊙ (Γi (x), Γi (y)) = 32 T◦ (x, y),                         i ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3},
(11.35)         T⊙ (∇i (x), ∇i (y)) = 21 T◦ (x, y),                          i ∈ {1, 2, 3},
                                          τ◦ (x◦x,y◦y)+|x◦y|2
(11.36)          T⊙ (Γi (x), Γj (y)) = −2 9|x|2 |y|2 −τ◦ (x,y)τ◦2 ,          i 6= j ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3},
                                               τ◦  τ◦

                                           3          |x◦y|2τ◦
(11.37)         T⊙ (∇i (x), ∇j (y)) = −                              ,
                                           2 4|x|2τ◦ |y|2τ◦ −τ◦ (x,y)2       i 6= j ∈ {1, 2, 3},
                                                                     2

(11.38)          T⊙ (∆(x), ∇i (y)) = −     6         |x|2τ◦ |y|2τ◦       ,   i ∈ {1, 2, 3},

for x, y ∈ A for which these expressions are defined. In particular:
    (1) If e ∈ I(A, ◦) is τ◦ -anisotropic, then T⊙ (∇i (e), ∇j (e)) = −(1/2)τ◦ (e, e)−1 and T⊙ (Γi (e), Γj (e)) =
        −(3/4)τ◦ (e, e)−1 .
    (2) If x ∈ A is τ◦ -anisotropic and y ∈ A is an eigenvector of L◦ (x) that is τ◦ -orthogonal to x and having
        eigenvalue λ, then T⊙ (∇i (x), ∇j (y)) = −(3λ2 /8)τ◦ (x, x)−1 .
    (3) If e ∈ I(A, ◦) is τ◦ -anisotropic and y ∈ A is an eigenvector of L◦ (e) that is τ◦ -orthogonal to e and
        having eigenvalue λ, then T⊙ (Γi (e), Γj (y)) = −(2λ(1+λ)/9)τ◦ (e, e)−1 = (2(1+λ)/(9(1−λ)))T◦(e, y).
    (4) If z ∈ N(A, ◦) is τ◦ -anisotropic and y ∈ A is an eigenvector of L◦ (e) that is τ◦ -orthogonal to e and
        having eigenvalue λ, then T⊙ (Γi (z), Γj (y)) = −(2λ2 /9)τ◦ (z, z)−1 = (2λ/(9(1 − λ)))T◦ (z, y).
    (5) If e ∈ I(A, ◦) and y is a vector τ◦ -orthogonal to e and contained in the λ-eigenspace of L◦ (e), then
        T⊙ (∆(e), w) = −(λ(2 + λ)/6)τ◦ (e, e)−1 = ((λ + 2)/(6(λ − 1))T◦ (e, y), for any w ∈ Z(y), where the
        second equality is valid provided λ 6= 1.
    (6) If z ∈ N(A, ◦) and y is an eigenvector of L◦ (z) that is τ◦ -orthogonal to z and having eigenvalue λ,
        then T⊙ (∆(z), w) = −(λ2 /6)τ◦ (z, z)−1 = (1/6)T◦ (z, y), for any w ∈ Z(y).

Proof. The identities (11.34) and (11.36) follow from (11.12) and (11.28). The identity (11.35) follows
from (11.12) and (11.29). Straightforward computations using (11.12), (11.26), and (11.30) yield (11.37) and
(11.38). Taking x = y = e in (11.36) and (11.37) yields claim (1). Specializing (11.37) yields (2). Specializing
(11.36) yields (3) and (4). Specializing (11.34)-(11.38) yields (5) and (6). By the proof of Lemma 11.20, if
L◦ (x)y = λy then any w ∈ Z(y) satisfies L⊙ (∆(x))w = −(λ/2)w. The identities (5) and (6) follow from
(7.1) using w ∈ Z(y) ⊂ ∆(A)⊥ .                                                                                

Corollary 11.22.
    (1) The triple algebra of a Euclidean Killing metrized commutative algebra does not have nonnegative
        intrinsic sectional nonassociativity.
    (2) The triple algebra of a Euclidean Killing metrized commutative algebra with nonpositive intrinsic
        sectional nonassociativity does not have nonpositive intrinsic sectional nonassociativity.
52                                              DANIEL J. F. FOX


Proof. A Euclidean Killing metrized commutative algebra contains an idempotent e, so, by (1) of Lemma
11.21, T⊙ (∇i (e), ∇j (e) < 0. This shows claim (1). By Lemma 2.13, a Euclidean Killing metrized commu-
tative algebra (A, ◦) contains a minimal idempotent e. If L◦ (e)z = λz and τ◦ (e, z) = 0, then nonpositive
sectional nonassociativity implies 0 ≥ τ◦ (e ◦ e, z ◦ z) − τ◦ (e ◦ z, e ◦ z) = λ(1 − λ)τ◦ (z, z), so λ ≤ 0 and
Π⊥ (e) ⊂ (−∞, 0]. If (A, ◦) is moreover exact, then L◦ (e) must have a negative eigenvalue λ. By claim (5)
of Lemma 11.9, if y ∈ A is an eigenvector of L◦ (e) with eigenvalue λ, then K(∆(e), ∇i (y)) > 0. This shows
claim (2).                                                                                                   

                                12. Concluding remarks and speculations
     This section records some remarks about topics not treated here and indicates directions for further study.
12.1. There are several crude dichotomies that help to organize Killing metrized exact commutative algebras.
There is the distinction between algebras whose automorphism groups are Lie groups and those whose
automorphism groups are discrete. There is the distinction between nonnegative and nonpositive sectional
nonassociativity (Norton inequality and reverse Norton inequality), or, more generally, lower bounds and
upper bounds on sectional nonassociativity. In very imprecise terms these distinctions overlap in that Lie
groups of automorphisms are associated with lower bounds on sectional nonassociativity (nonnegativity)
while upper bounds on sectional nonassociativity (nonpositivity) tend to force discreteness of automorphism
groups. Substance is given to such claims by the example of formally real simple Euclidean Jordan algebras
and their deunitalizations (Lemma 9.14 and Corollary 9.19) and results such as Lemma 8.9. From this
point of view that Griess algebras whose automorphism groups are finite satisfy the Norton inequality
(nonnegativity) could appear somewhat anomalous, but could be explained via a more refined study of
the relation between sectional nonassociativity and the spectra of the multiplication endomorphisms of
idempotents. The machinery developed by Tkachev in [80] facilitates precise formulations, and these ideas
will be pursued in joint work with Tkachev.
12.2. It is implicit in the work of R. Hamilton and G. Huisken on the Ricci flow that a certain commutative
multiplication on the space of metric curvature tensors discovered by Hamilton makes this space into a
metrized commutative R-algebra. This is explained in [25], where it is shown that the subspaces of Weyl
and Kähler Weyl curvature tensors constitute subalgebras that are Killing metrized and exact. This an
interesting family of examples. Among the examples considered in the present paper they most closely
resemble the deunitalizations of simple Euclidean Jordan algebras in that their automorphism groups are
Lie groups and consequently their idempotents constitute submanifolds (these are studied in detail in [25]).
Several interesting questions arise:
    (1) Obtain estimates of the sectional nonassociativities of the algebras of Weyl curvature tensors. The
         author expects there is a lower bound similar to that in Corollary 9.19.
    (2) As vector spaces the spaces of Weyl and Kähler Weyl curvature tensors are naturally identified with
         the second Cartan powers of the Lie algebras so(n) and su(n). There should be a general construction
         of a commutative product on the second Cartan power of a compact simple real Lie algebra g that
         makes it a Killing metrized exact commutative algebra and that recovers the Hamilton product when
         g is so(n) o su(n).
    (3) The geometric motivations lead to considering these algebras over R. It should be interesting to
         study them over more general fields, starting with C.
12.3. In [27] it was shown how to associate with a tight frame in a Euclidean vector space a harmonic
cubic polynomial. This polynomial determines an exact metrized commutative algebra. For a balanced
tight two-distance unit norm frame, the author has shown that the associated exact metrized commutative
algebra is Killing metrized. A tight two-distance frame determines a strongly regular graph. In fact these
combinatorial objects are essentially equivalent; [6, Theorem 1.2] characterizes nonequiangular tight two-
distance frames in terms of strongly regular graphs and [87] charaterizes equiangular tight frames in similar
terms. A strongly regular graph in turn determines an association scheme [5, 15]. When it satisfies some
hypothesis, a joint eigenspace of the Bose-Mesner algebra of an association scheme carries a structure of
                  COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                    53


a commutative nonassociative algebra, called a Norton algebra, introduced in [14, Section 6], [15]. For a
balanced tight two-distance unit frame, the algebra determined by the cubic polynomial of the frame as in
[27] can be shown to be isomorphic to the Norton algebra of the associated association scheme (the author
did not understand this when he wrote [27]). The conclusion that these Norton algebras are Killing metrized
is new, and the notion of sectional nonassociativity offers interesting possibilities for studying these Norton
algebras. These examples are particularly interesting because explicit computations are viable because of
the detailed combinatorial information available regarding strongly regular graphs. However, developing the
background necessary for their presentation to a readership not specializing in the study of the relevant
combinatorial objects requires considerable space, and so these results will be treated in a companion paper.
   A general question suggested by these examples is for what metrized commutative exact algebras does
some subset of I(A, ◦) have some particular combinatorial structure. For example, when does some subset
of I(A, ◦) constitute a two-distance tight frame? When it does, to what extent does such combinatorial
structure on its idempotents determine the algebra up to isomorphism?

12.4. It would be interesting to adapt the notions considered here to work over fields of positive charac-
teristic, in particular over finite fields. Interesting examples are known. The Harada-Norton group is the
sporadic simple group HN of order 214 · 36 · 56 · 7 · 11 · 19. In [71], A. Ryba constructed a 133-dimensional
metrized commutative algebra defined over the finite field F5 and it follows from Theorem 1, Lemma 9.1(ii),
and Theorems 5 and 6 of [71] that this algebra is Killing metrized with automorphism group equal to HN .
That the invariant bilinear form equals twice the Killing form is claim (ii) of Lemma 9.1, while the invariance
is Theorem 6.

    Appendix A. The Chern-do Carmo-Kobayashi inequality over real Hurwitz algebras
   For a real Hurwitz algebra k, let M(n, k) be the space of n × n matrices over k. Let Herm(n, k) = {X ∈
M(n, k) : X̄ t = X} where the conjugate transpose X → X̄ t is induced by the involution of k fixing the real
subfield. The Frobenius bilinear form f (X, Y ) = Re tr X̄ t Y = 12 tr(X̄ t Y + Ȳ t X) is symmetric [24, Proposition
V.2.1] and positive definite (this is true for k = O even if n > 3).
   The Böttcher-Wenzel inequality, first proved for real symmetric matrices by Chern-do Carmo-Kobayashi
[17], is an optimal bound on the Frobenius norm of the commutator of two complex matrices in terms
of the product of the Frobenius norms of the matrices, that is stronger than the bound obtained via a
naive application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. This appendix recalls these inequalities, shows that
the Chern-do Carmo-Kobayashi inequality holds for Herm(n, H) and partially for Herm(3, O), and makes
some remarks about the proofs of both. These refinements are used in the main text to obtain bounds on
sectional nonassociativities. Theorem A.2 and the variant Lemma A.3, valid over a real Hurwitz algebra, are
used in the proof of Lemma 9.14 to estimate the sectional nonassociativity of the deunitalization of a simple
Euclidean Jordan algebra. In a sense, this amounts to reinterpreting the Böttcher-Wenzel and Chern-do
Carmo-Kobayashi inequalities as upper bounds on sectional nonassociativity.
Lemma A.1. Let k be a real Hurwitz algebra. For n ≥ 1, the number
                                             n |[X,Y ]|2                     o
(A.1)                W = W(M(n, k)) = sup |X|2 |Y |f2 : X, Y ∈ M(n, k) \ {0}
                                                            f   f


is finite and
(A.2)                         |[X, Y ]|2f ≤ W(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ) ≤ W|X|2f |Y |2f ,

for all X, Y ∈ M(n, k). If |[X, Y ]|2f ≤ W(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ) then P = |Y |f X − |X|f Y and Q = |Y |f X +
|X|f Y satisfy |[P, Q]|2f = W|P |2f |Q|2f .

Proof. That W(M(n, k)) is finite follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. The following argument from
the proof of [89, Theorem 2.2] shows that the apparently weaker inequality |[X, Y ]|2f ≤ W(M(n, k))|X|2f |Y |2f
implies the first inequality of (A.2). If either X or Y equals 0 there is nothing to show, so suppose X 6= 0
54                                                     DANIEL J. F. FOX


and Y 6= 0. Take P = |Y |f X − |X|f Y and Q = |Y |f X + |X|f Y . Then [P, Q] = 2|X|f |Y |f [X, Y ], and
straightforward computation shows
(A.3)            |[X, Y ]|2f = 4|X|21 |Y |2 |[P, Q]|2 ≤ 2|X|21 |Y |2 |P |2f |Q|2f = 2(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ).
                                    f    f                    f   f


If there is equality in the first inequality of (A.2), then (A.3) shows that |[P, Q]|2f = W|P |2f |Q|2f .            

Theorem A.2 ([17], [12]). There holds W(M(n, C)) = 2. That is for X, Y ∈ M(n, C),
(A.4)                             |[X, Y ]|2f ≤ 2(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ) ≤ 2|X|2f |Y |2f ,

for the inner product f (X, Y ) = Re tr X̄ t Y .
     (1) If either X, Y ∈ Herm(n, C) or X, Y ∈ su(n), then |[X, Y ]|2f = 2|X|2f |Y |2f if and only if X and Y are
         simultaneously unitarily conjugate to scalar multiples of e11 − enn and e1n + en1 .
     (2) If either X, Y ∈ Herm(n, C) or X, Y ∈ su(n), then |[X, Y ]|2f = 2(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ) if and only
         if X and Y either are linearly dependent over C or are simultaneously unitarily conjugate to scalar
         multiples of e11 − enn and e1n + en1 .
Proof. The inequality (A.4) as such was first proved for real symmetric matrices, with a characterization of
the case of equality |[X, Y ]|2f = 2|X|2f |Y |2f , as [17, Lemma 1]. It was proved for M(n, C) in full generality by Z.
Lu in [56] and as stated in the proof of [12, Theorem 3.1]. A nice exposition of the proof of (A.4), following [4],
can be found in [90, chapter 9]. Its history is somewhat complicated; see [4, 11, 12, 16, 56, 57, 58, 90] among
many references. Characterizations of equality in (A.4) are given in various references, for instance [12]. Here
these characterizations are needed when X, Y ∈ Herm(n, C). Since multiplication by i is an isometric real
linear isomorphism from su(n) to Herm(n, C), the validity of either of (1) or (2) for X and Y both Hermitian
or both skew-Hermitian implies its validity for the other class. The characterization of equality in (1) for
Hermitian matrices is proved as it is in [17] for Herm(n, R); see the proof of Lemma A.3 below. Suppose
there holds |[X, Y ]|2f = 2(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ) in (A.4) for linearly independent X, Y ∈ Herm(n, C). Then
(A.3) shows that the Hermitian matrices P and Q satisfy |[P, Q]|2 = 2|P |2f |Q|2f , so, by (1), are simultaneously
unitarily conjugate to (nonzero) scalar multiples of e11 −enn and e1n +en1 . Since this implies that |P |2f = |Q|2f ,
from X = P2|X|
             +Q
                 and Y = Q−P                                                                           2         2   2
                             2|Y | it follows that f (X, Y ) = 0, so, by (A.3), there holds |[X, Y ]|f = 2|X|f |Y |f .
By (1), X and Y are simultaneously unitarily conjugate to scalar multiples of e11 − enn and e1n + en1 . This
proves (2).                                                                                                          

   The inequality (A.4) is not valid over H and O. Taking X = iI and Y = jI ∈ M(n, H) shows W(M(n, k)) ≥ 4
when k ∈ {H, O}. In [33, Theorem 3.1] it is shown that W(M(n, H)) = 4 and the equality case is characterized.
Nonetheless, the proof of (A.4) given in [17] for Herm(n, R) adapts for Herm(n, k) over any real Hurwitz
algebra k ∈ {R, C, H, O}. Over R or C, because a Hermitian matrix is unitarily diagonalizable, it suffices to
prove the claim in the case one of the matrices is diagonal. This proof requires the prinicipal axis theorem,
which is valid over H and O, and one has to check that the noncommutativity of H and O does not effect
the rest of the argument. This works over H, but the reduction via diagonalization faces an obstacle over
O, namely that, for X, Y ∈ Herm(n, k), the inequality (A.4) is not manifestly invariant with respect to the
action of Aut(Herm(n, k), ⋆). Over associative k, Aut(Herm(n, k)) preserves the commutator of the matrix
product on M(n, k) restricted to Herm(n, k), but when k = O, in which case Aut(Herm(3, O)) is the compact
form of the simple real Lie group of type F4 [72], it is not clear to the author whether this is true. However the
partial result, that (A.4) holds when X is diagonal, is true, and this suffices for the application to sectional
nonassociativity in the proof of Lemma 9.14.
Lemma A.3. Let k be a real Hurwitz algebra. Let f (X, Y ) = Re tr X̄ t Y on M(n, k).
     (1) If k is associative, for all X, Y ∈ Herm(n, k) there holds
(A.5)                             |[X, Y ]|2f ≤ 2(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ) ≤ 2|X|2f |Y |2f .
     (2) If k = O and n = 3, (A.5) holds for all Y ∈ Herm(3, O) and all diagonal X ∈ Herm(3, O).
                   COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRAS WITH NONDEGENERATE INVARIANT TRACE FORM                                                                  55


    (3) The equality |[X, Y ]|2f = 2|X|2f |Y |2f holds in (A.5) (where, if k = O and n = 3, X is assumed
        diagonal) if and only if X and Y are simultaneously equivalent via an automorphism of the Jordan
        algebra (Herm(n, k), ⋆) to real multiples of e11 − enn and e1n + en1 .
    (4) The equality |[X, Y ]|2f = 2(|X|2f |Y |2f − f (X, Y )2 ) holds in (A.5) (where, if k = O and n = 3, X is
        assumed diagonal) if and only if X and Y are either linearly dependent over k or are simultaneously
        equivalent via an automorphism of the Jordan algebra (Herm(n, k), ⋆) to real multiples of e11 − enn
        and e1n + en1 .
Proof. Suppose X ∈ Herm(n, k) is diagonal, where n = 3 if k = O. Since X is Hermitian, its entries are real.
Let xi = Xii . Then [X, Y ]ij = xi Yij − Yij xj = (xi − xj )Yij , the last equality because xj is real, so in the
center of k. Now the proof goes through as in [17]:

Remark A.4. Observe that although the same difficulty related to diagonalization occurs in the proof of
Lemma 9.14 as occurs in the proof of Lemma A.3, the end result in Lemma 9.14 is stated in terms manifestly
invariant with respect to Aut(Herm(n, k)), and as a result takes the same form whether or not k is associative.
This observation suggests that the upper bound on sectional nonassociativity, which is closely related to the
commutator bound, is the more natural bound to consider, at least from the algebraic point of view, although
it could also simply reflect a technical deficiency in the proof of Lemma A.3.                               ⊳
Remark A.5. It would be useful to know whether (A.5) is true for Herm(3, O). This seems likely. More,
generally, it would be interesting to know whether (A.5) is true for Herm(n, O) for n > 3. This would follow
from a principal axis theorem and a characterization of the automorphism group, but it appears that neither
is known. See [92] for what is known in this regard.                                                            ⊳
Remark A.6. Although this does not seem to be realized widely, for skew-Hermitian matrices the inequality
(A.4) is closely related to Vinberg’s results on invariant norms on compact simple Lie algebras in [86] applied
in the special case of su(n). Precisely, Vinberg shows that, for an invariant norm, || · || on a compact
simple Lie algebra g the quantity θ(x) = sup06=y∈g ||[x,y]||
                                                        ||y|| does not depend on the choice of invariant norm,
and equals the spectral norm of adg (x). Since by definition θ([x, y]) ≤ θ(x)θ(y), taking g to be a compact
simple Lie algebra of matrices, e.g. skew-symmetric or skew-Hermitian matrices, and taking || · || to be the
Frobenius norm there results |[X, Y ]|f ≤ θ(X)|Y |f . It suffices then to check that the spectral norm of adg (X)
56                                                   DANIEL J. F. FOX

                    √
is no greater than 2|X|f . In the case g = su(n) this can be shown as follows. Since any skew-Hermitian
matrix is unitarily conjugate to a diagonal matrix and the spectral norms of the adjoint representations of
unitarily conjugate matrices are the same, it suffices to consider the case of diagonal X ∈ su(n). In this case
the nonzero eigenvalues of adsu(n) (X) have the form λi − λj where λ1 , . . . , λn are the diagonal elements of
X. Since |λi − λj |2 ≤ 2|λ|2 ≤ 2|X|2f , where λ is the element of X with the greatest modulus, the spectral
                                         √
norm of adsu(n) (X) is no greater than 2|X|f . In the case of g = so(n), something equivalent is shown in
[9].                                                                                                          ⊳
